Session 69 · 2026-09-05 · The buildout catches up with thirteen sessions of shipped work

The maintainer asked whether the scheduler buildout had been kept up. It had not, and the gap was measurable rather than a feeling: thirty requirements, R-306 to R-335, belonged to no stage. The highest requirement any stage claimed was R-305; the stage list ended at S25 while the sessions ran to 68. Every one of those thirty had a requirement row, a session entry and a test --- what was missing was only the layer that says which of them were ONE JOB. EIGHT STAGES, S26 TO S33, GROUPED FROM THE REQUIREMENTS' OWN CLAIMS AND THE SESSIONS THAT SHIPPED THEM: the status columns that never meant anything (R-322-324); standard cycle times and the roll-up that had to learn adding up is not always right (R-315-317, R-319); one shell, one field, one chevron, each with an audit forbidding a second (R-311, R-313, R-314, R-318); Edit as one door on both screens that have one (R-306, R-307); the plant filter reaching past the admin screen (R-308, R-310, R-312, R-325, R-334); the Activity log (R-328-330); settings answered for a plant (R-309, R-320, R-331-333, R-335); and things that can be brought to an end (R-326, R-327). S24 gains R-321, the sign-in redirect it actually delivered. THE GROUPING IS A JUDGEMENT AND IS MARKED AS ONE. Each new card's state says it was written on 5 Sept rather than when the work shipped, which is the footing S25's own card already sits on. If any of the eight cuts the work in the wrong place, moving a requirement between two cards costs nothing --- the requirements and their tests are the load-bearing half and were never wrong. NINE SESSIONS NOW NAME THE STAGE THEY SHIPPED, which is the link that had been empty. shipped: [] on thirteen consecutive sessions was the visible symptom and nobody read it as one, including me. AND THE QUEUE HAD GONE STALE IN A WAY THAT COULD HAVE COST REAL WORK. "A way to CHOOSE warn or block for eligibility" was still listed as next, and its own note described the missing piece as a Settings control plus one write RPC --- which is what sessions 62-63 shipped. Checked by reading SettingsPanel rather than the plan: there is a live <select> writing eligibility_policy. It is removed, with the reason, under the same 4 Sept convention that records why each queue item went. THE ITEM BESIDE IT IS STILL GENUINELY OPEN and was checked the same way, so the sweep does not only delete: nothing in src/features/board/ reads expiringSkills, so an expired certificate really is still invisible on the board until the server refuses the save. Left where it is. meta.baseline moves off 1788 tests in 60 files, which it had read since the file was assembled, to 2106 in 75, and meta.tip to c24bc69 --- the commit those numbers were measured on. 1Phase 1 — E2E test pass (Playwright) + load sanity
Unchecked Phase 1 item.
2⛔ A DEAD END ON THE BOARD: an expired certificate is invisible until the server refuses the save
⚠️ MEASURED, AND THIS IS A DEFECT RATHER THAN THE PHASE 2 POLISH THE LINE USED TO DESCRIBE. The check already exists end to end on the SERVER: `check_eligibility` computes `expiring_skills` (`expires_at < upper(p_timerange)`) and folds it into `eligible`, and `create_assignment` refuses on it — under `block` outright, under `warn` unless an override is supplied. THE BOARD DOES NOT ASK THAT QUESTION. `CreatePopover` derives `ineligible` from `!o.skillIds.includes(s.id)` — whether the person HOLDS the training at all — and expiry is never considered, so somebody whose certificate lapsed last year shows as perfectly eligible, gets no warning and is offered no override tick. Pressing Create then raises `not_eligible … override required under warn policy`, and because the client never decided an override was needed there is no way to supply one: the assignment cannot be made through the board at all. The api layer already parses `expiringSkills` (`shapes.ts`) and nothing in `src/features/board/` reads it, which is the whole of the gap. This is CLAUDE.md section 4's rule with the arrow reversed — a screen OFFERING what the server refuses.

Scheduler buildout

The scheduler — stages in order

Stages run in order — each one either unblocks the next or has to land before something risky is switched on.

Open — this is what is left

24

The front door: getting people into the system at all

Parked on your call — capability first. Recorded here so it can't go missing again, because it already did once. You asked where somebody searches to add a person, and whether they'd have to be in the database already. They would, and nothing in the app puts them there: no sign-up, no invitation, no password reset, and no sign-in screen at all. The only way anyone logs in today is a developer-only switcher that never ships in a real build.

It splits in two, and the halves are very different sizes. A real sign-in screen is small — one browser call, no server piece, probably a single sitting. Invitations are the bigger half and need a piece of privileged server-side code this project doesn't have yet.

What is left in this stage

  • A sign-in screen — DONE (Sep 1). Email and password, a gate that remembers where you were going, and sign out.
  • The dead end where somebody signs in but belongs to no company — DONE (Sep 1). It now shows a plain no-access page instead of nothing useful.
  • Invitations (P1-6c). The big half: it needs a privileged server-side Edge Function, and this project has none at all yet.
  • Password reset and change. Same shape as the sign-in screen.

What is left is the bigger half: invitations (P1-6c), which needs the project's first server-side Edge Function, plus password reset and change (P1-6d). The sign-in half is done.

★ DONE (Sep 1) — the small half is in. There is a real sign-in screen now: email and password, a route gate that sends you to sign in and then on to where you were headed, a way to sign out, and a plain dead-end for somebody who gets in but belongs to no company. The developer-only switcher is no longer the only door. Sign-in (P1-6b) and the no-access dead end shipped in session 19 (1 Sept); invitations (P1-6c) and password reset (P1-6d) are not built, so the stage stays open.

Each site runs itself

A site admin does everything inside their own plant — the tree, the level names, who gets in, the skills a cell needs, the shift pattern a line runs — and provably nothing outside it. A company admin still goes anywhere. Creating, removing and moving a plant stay company-wide, because those aren't things you do inside a site.

Finished, applied and green — your 502 matched the prediction exactly. 40 new checks, 221 database checks in total, 34 of 35 deliberate breakages caught. Three things turned up that weren't in the plan; they're below.

8

Drag to reorder, on screen

The gesture you tried in your first minute. Drop on the top or bottom half of a row to place above or below it; drop on a row that could be a parent to move it inside. The whole row becomes draggable, not just the handle, and the level list gets the same treatment in the same build.

Built — the rules, the gesture and both surfaces. An agent built the on-screen half from a brief; I checked its work rather than its report. 35 deliberate breakages across the new pieces, all caught. It found a bug that had been shipping since the drag first landed — below. Then I closed the last thing it flagged: a drop that would land a row exactly where it already sits is no longer offered at all.

§19.54 · migration 0022

Delivers R-210, R-238, R-239

DoneMine
12

The drag on the levels list — now grabbable, and there *was* a bug behind it

You said it still isn't there. It was — and my own roadmap agreed with you, which is the actual story. Whole-row dragging on the level list was built in stage 08: pointer capture, a movement threshold, an insertion caret, the lot. The queue entry for it was never ticked off, so for five days two of us believed it was unwritten.

Why it felt absent, measured rather than guessed: rendering one level row and testing every pixel against the code's own rule, 21 pixels out of 526 across the middle of the row would start a drag — three seven-pixel gaps between the controls — and nothing marked them. No grip, and the cursor never changed; the only place it did change was over the buttons, which are the parts you can't drag.

Now: a grip at the left of every row (the same one the node tree uses), a hand cursor on the row, and a soft tint when you hover it. 21 pixels becomes 53, and the hand cursor now appears on exactly the pixels that work and nowhere else — the text box says "text", the buttons say "click", the rest says "grab". Touch gets a path for the first time too, as a side effect of matching the tree.

You told me to look for a bug behind the affordance once it was grabbable. There was one. With no way to drive your running app, I did the next honest thing: rendered the real row with the real stylesheet, drove the real code over it at 672 pointer positions, and fed every result through the real reordering function. Two answers came back, and they were opposite. The maths was right — 528 out of 528 drops landed exactly where the green line promised. And 231 of those 528 — 44% — drew a green line that promised a move and then did nothing at all.

It's the same defect I fixed on the node tree two weeks ago, still alive on this screen because the two lists keep their own copies of the rule — and nobody could find it here, because nobody could grab a row. Dragging a level, the gap directly above it and the gap directly below it are both where it already is. Those two now behave properly: the whole of the row above means "put it above", the whole of the row below means "put it below", and the row you're dragging shows no line at all. Re-measured after: 396 live pixels, none dead, no disagreements.

Done. 25 new checks, 11 deliberate breakages, 10 caught and 1 proved harmless with a check written to pin why it's harmless. Two more things came out of just looking at it: the hover tint was landing one pixel from the green line and made it read as a border instead of an insertion point, and the grip has been effectively invisible on both admin screens since it shipped — measured at 1.29 to 1 against its background, where 3 to 1 is the floor. Both fixed. Still not proved: nothing has driven your running app. If you connect Claude to your browser, that gap closes completely and I can drive it myself.

§19.56 · P1-6e

Delivers R-228, R-230

DoneMine
13

One drag surface, two screens — “shouldn’t this be done by default?”

Your question, and it was the right one. You looked at the node tree and the levels list side by side and said the drag styling didn’t match, that it should match by default, and that we were reinventing rather than reusing. All three were true, and the third is the one that matters.

Measured, the two screens disagreed in five places at once: two different hover colours; real padding on one and a fake shadow imitating padding on the other; two copies of the same fourteen-line grip block; two copies of the same fade; and the grip itself in a different place on each. Every line in both files was correct. The defect was that there were two of them. And one of the two hover colours was the exact three-unit grey I’d already measured as rendering nothing — so “make them match” could just as easily have meant copying an invisible tint onto the second screen.

So the fix isn’t matching them. It’s making it impossible to mismatch. Every drag colour is now a named value in one place, and the shared rules — grip, hand cursor, hover tint, row padding, the fade, the text-selection lock — live in one file that both screens pull from. What’s left in each screen is only what genuinely differs: the tree’s insertion line indents to the depth of the thing you’re dragging, the levels list’s spans the row. That’s geometry, not taste.

And the grip moved, because you were right about that too — sitting next to the up/down arrows put two ways of moving the same row side by side, so the pair read as one control with a broken half. It now sits second from the right, immediately before the row’s own ×, exactly where the tree puts its grip before the ⋮. A left-edge grip is the more common convention in the abstract; matching the screen next to it wins, because you look at these two together.

Done. 12 new checks and 10 deliberate breakages, all 10 caught — the checks fail the build if either screen ever grows its own copy of a shared rule again, or reaches past the named colours to a raw one. That is the part that makes “by default” true rather than aspirational. Draggable pixels across the middle of a level row: 21 before, 61 now.

§19.58 · D100

Delivers R-D100, R-230

DoneMine
14

The shared lists get an owner — and it turns out to gate everything else

Operators, products, skills and shift patterns become a site's own, the way the tree and the level names now are. Entries with no owner stay company-wide.

I moved this up your list, and here is the reason it earned the jump. You asked whether I could build Shifts, Operators, Products and Import in parallel. Checking that properly turned up something neither of us had looked at: today a site admin can edit no operator, no product and no shift pattern — and the admin page lets them in anyway. Those rules still ask the old company-wide question. So switching any of those four screens on before this change ships a screen that opens for a site admin and quietly throws away every edit they make. That is the same lesson as stage 11, pointing the other way: stage 11 was anything you hide, the server must refuse; this is anything you show, the server must allow — and this one is worse, because it looks like it works.

And your two answers land here. “The shift pattern will be per-site, we can have defaults but I’d rather the site admin set them up for their site, same thing for colours.” So shift patterns join the same change — which also settles a contradiction I’d found between two of my own documents, one saying company-wide and the other saying per-site, with nothing to break the tie. “We can have defaults” is already how this change works: an entry with nobody’s name on it stays company-wide, so you seed a standard pattern once and each site adds its own alongside.

Colours the same way, and it finally fixes the positional accident: a product gets a colour of its own instead of inheriting whichever slot it happens to sit in, stored as a name from the palette rather than a raw hex so a theme change stays one edit, with a sensible default so an untouched product still looks right. Whoever owns the product sets its colour — the site admin for their own, the company admin for the shared ones. What I deliberately did not build: a per-site override of a shared product’s colour. That would make one product two different colours on two boards, which is the exact confusion a colour exists to prevent.

Built, and four things were wrong with it before it shipped — not one of them found by reading it. A permission trigger was answering the question itself instead of letting the rule answer, so a refusal came back saying “that plant doesn’t exist” about a plant that plainly does. The colour palette shipped with eight colours against a stylesheet that defines four, so half of them would have rendered as no colour at all — found by a test my own notes had argued was unnecessary. A product’s colour could be blanked. And the one that matters most: I handed the finished file to a separate agent whose only instruction was “break this”, and it found a hole I had read past three times — the colour picker could be asked about another company’s products. Its suggested fix was also wrong, and measuring that is what produced the right one. That third pass is now a standing part of how anything I build myself gets checked.

One deliberate limit, stated so it isn’t mistaken for an oversight: nothing existing was handed to a site. Every operator, product, skill and shift pattern that exists today stays company-wide, because deciding they belong to Plant 1 is a decision you never made. The practical consequence is that after you run the CLI, nothing looks different — a site admin still sees the same shared lists, because that is all there is. Two sample rows owned by Plant 2 in the demo data would make it visible, and that is a small next step rather than part of this change.

Done. One database change, not four, carrying both of your decisions. 315 database checks pass (271 before), 28 deliberate breakages, 24 caught — the other four proved harmless, each with the rule it depends on now pinned by its own check. The app test count does not move: still 713. Nothing on screen reads the new columns yet, so a different number would mean something else broke. Costs you a CLI run and a commit.

§19.59 · §19.60 · D101 · D102 · migration 0023

DoneMine
15

Moving something to a different rung — and the two things the database had been saying wrong

The buttons for stage 03's second half: when a plant was set up with something at the wrong rung, this moves it up or down and brings everything under it along. Drag moves a thing sideways; this moves it up and down.

The menu says what it will make the thing, not what the operation is called. Not “Promote” — “Make this a Department”, using the words your own structure uses for its rungs. And when it can’t be done, the entry stays visible and greyed with the reason underneath rather than quietly vanishing: “Move to…” can shorten a list because there is a list, but this is one thing to click, and hiding it leaves someone with a tree they can’t fix and no idea why.

And building the screen found two things the database had been getting wrong since stage 03 — both invisible to every test that existed. The reason they were invisible is worth more than the bugs: every one of those 33 tests asked what a legal move does, and not one asked what a refusal looks like.

First: moving something into a place that already has something of that name came back as a raw database error with no explanation attached at all — the screen would have shown a line of Postgres jargon. And I had measured that exact failure a day before writing stage 03, written down what to do about it, and then not done it. The note was in the design document the whole time. Nothing noticed, because nothing was looking at refusals.

Second, and worse: the most important refusal this feature makes — “you can’t move this, there are three shifts booked on it” — was packaged in a shape the app can’t read. It would have reached you as “Something went wrong. Please try again.” That is the whole point of the feature arriving as a shrug.

Done, plus a small database change to fix both. 332 database checks (315 before), 11 deliberate breakages, 7 caught, the other four harmless with the rule each depends on pinned by its own check. On the screen side, 36 new checks and 15 deliberate breakages, all 15 caught. Your test count moves: 713 → 751.

16

Shifts, Operators, Products — three screens, and the answer to your parallel question

You asked whether I could spin up agents to work on these at once. I measured it instead of guessing: four read-only survey agents, one per screen, running simultaneously, each told to read the database files as the authority and to hand back an exhaustive list of every file it would have to touch. All four came back, they agree with each other, and the answer is a qualified yes.

Four agents building four screens at once would collide, and not on anything interesting. Every one of them flips the same switch in the same navigation file and adds a block to the same list. Every one of them adds its stylesheet to an audit list — and to a second hard-coded copy of that same list in a test, which fails the build if it's missed, and which one of my own briefs already missed once. Every one adds a line to the same exports file, appends to the same two planning documents, and needs the same database change from stage 14.

But every single one of those collisions is in a file I can edit once, up front. So the shape is: stage 14 lands, you run the CLI, and then one small commit that pre-seats the shared files — all four panels created and wired in, all four stylesheets created and listed in both places, all four data modules created and exported. After that each agent only ever creates its own files, and the lanes really do run at once.

The pre-seat commit is done — that is what this handoff is. Every one of those shared files is now edited, once. Four empty panels exist, four empty data modules exist, and both audit lists know about them. Nothing changed on screen: the four rail buttons are still greyed with their “soon” labels, because a section only turns on when its own panel says it is ready.

And that last part is the bit worth keeping. The switch that turns a screen on used to live in the shell, next to the other five; it now lives inside each panel. So a screen cannot be switched on unless there is a panel behind it — not because someone remembers, but because the switch is part of the thing it switches on. The same move as the drag colours in stage 13: make the rule structural rather than remembered.

Done, and the three lanes have now run — see the next stage. Each runs in three waves: a read-only survey, then the build, then a review by a different agent that did not write it — that third wave is what caught the cross-tenant hole in stage 14, so it is not optional. Import is not one of the parallel lanes; see the next stage for why. 6 new checks, 6 deliberate breakages, all 6 caught.

§19.57 · §19.62 · §19.63

Delivers R-239, R-246, R-247

DoneMine
17

The three screens — built at once, then deliberately attacked

Shifts, Operators and Products were built in parallel, exactly as stage 16 said they could be, and the pre-seat held: 21 files changed, every one of them a file its own lane owned, and not a single shared file touched twice. That is the parallel question answered in practice rather than on paper.

What each screen does. Shift patterns is clock times and nothing else — a shift that runs past midnight shows its end marked “+1d”, and a break at quarter past midnight is inside a night shift rather than twenty hours outside it. Operators leads with your own question: where can this person work, and why not. A list of places, a tick or a cross against each, and the reason beside the cross — “missing Welding”, “Forklift expires 3 Sep”. Tickets are underneath, because they are the mechanism, not the vocabulary. Change the date you are asking about and the answer changes, which is the thing a stored yes/no could never do. Products shows each product in its own colour — the one the database picked for it, not its position in a list — and puts deactivate before delete, because anything that has ever been scheduled can never be truly deleted at all.

Then I had them attacked, and this is the part worth reading. The three lanes came back green: the type checker clean, the linter clean, all three test suites passing, and twenty deliberate breakages of my own, all twenty caught. I had rendered the screens and looked at the picture. By every instrument I own, this was finished work.

Eleven defects were still in it. I sent one reviewer per screen — agents that had not written a line of it — and told each one to hunt a specific kind of mistake rather than to “review the code”. Every finding was then reproduced here before anything was changed, and two of their claims did not survive that step. The four that mattered are in the list below.

The lesson I am taking from it is uncomfortable and worth saying plainly: my own deliberate breakages only test what I had already thought of. All twenty of mine were caught. Nine of theirs went straight through a green suite. A breakage list written by the person who wrote the code is measured against that person’s own picture of it — the same picture that left the holes. The review wave cost less than any of the three builds.

Done and handed over. 10 new checks — 1030 to 1040 — and nine deliberate breakages that a green suite had ignored: eight now caught, one measured genuinely harmless and written down as such rather than left as a number. No database change. ⚠️ Five things I found are deliberately NOT fixed, because each needs your call rather than a patch — the first one is at the top of the “what turned up” list and it is the largest single thing outstanding in the project.

Delivers R-248, R-249, R-253

DoneMine
18

You opened the screens, and found six things in ten minutes

Five of the six are not mistakes in what the code computes. They are things the screen never offered — and that is a class of defect no test can find, because a test can only check things somebody thought to build. This is worth saying plainly: the three screens had passed a type check, a linter, three test suites, twenty deliberate breakages of my own, eleven found by reviewers, and a picture I had studied. Then you clicked on them.

Breaks were a sentence; they are a table now. Name, clock, length, actions — four columns you can read down, which is what the two real questions need (“which is the long one”, “do these two collide”). My first attempt at it stretched the name column across the whole panel and put “Lunch” six hundred pixels from “10:00–10:30”, which is a flex row wearing a table’s clothes. The render caught that; nothing else would have.

A break can be edited now — and the write to do it already existed. This is the uncomfortable one. updateBreak had been built, tested, given its own error handling, and called from nowhere on screen. So the only way to move a break by five minutes was to delete it and retype it, which also silently lost its name. A destructive action offered for a non-destructive intent. There is a cheap check for this shape and I have now been bitten by it twice: look for something the code exports that nothing imports.

A pattern row now looks like it opens. It was a button with no border and no background — pixel-identical to the plain text beside it — with the entire shift list of that pattern hidden behind it. You found it by clicking on the off-chance; a new user would not have. It has a caret that turns, a name that underlines when you point at it, and a “show shifts” hint.

And the colour is yours to set. The original decision was that a product’s colour is chosen for it and never changes — and the reasoning was about the system not changing its mind behind your back, which is still right. It said nothing about you deciding. The swatch is now a button that opens the palette; creating a product still picks a colour automatically, and nothing re-picks on a rename. The hint under the form used to end “and never changes afterwards”, which had to change in the same breath — a hint describing the old behaviour is worse than none, because it tells someone the control they are looking at does not exist.

Done and handed over. 6 new checks — 1040 to 1046 — and a mutation found one more hole while I was writing them: two different problems on the same shift were being treated as the same problem, so a shift that already had a naming complaint could be given an illegal time and save silently. No database change.

19

“Belongs to” becomes a place in the hierarchy, not just a plant

Your question: a product, a ticket or a shift pattern might belong to one line rather than a whole plant — how do we say so, and have everything under that line inherit it? Today the picker offers only “company-wide” or a plant name.

I drew the two things that could mean and you picked the larger one: where something belongs decides where it is offered, not just who may edit it. A product on Line 1 appears on Line 1’s cells and nowhere else; company-wide stays the everywhere fallback. It is the same “nearest one above you wins” rule shift patterns already use.

People were deliberately left out here — and you overturned that the same day, correctly. See stage 20. What I had argued at the time: Where someone can work is already answered by their tickets against what a cell requires. A second rule that could disagree with that would mean two systems refusing for different reasons with nothing on screen able to reconcile them. So a person’s “belongs to” filters the roster you see and nothing else.

It is cheaper than it sounds. Nothing has to be migrated — everything currently set to a plant or to company-wide stays valid, so this only widens what the picker will accept. The permission half already works for any level. The board can do the filtering itself from data it already loads. What genuinely has to be built is the picker becoming a tree, and every list that offers a choice learning to ask “which cell am I on?” first.

Built, and the “Belongs to” box is the whole tree now — company-wide, then every plant, department, line and cell, indented so you can see where you are. Products, trainings and shift patterns all use it. Colours are yours too: the four presets are still one click, and there is a picker and a hex field beside them for the case where four is not enough. A colour is still chosen automatically when you create a product, and still never moves on its own.

The database change is a widening, which sounds safe and is the risky kind. Everything that was legal before is still legal, so every existing test passes no matter how far the change went — which is why more than half the new tests check what must still be refused: another company’s node, a supervisor who is not an admin, and five near-miss colour spellings. Deliberately breaking one of those found a real hole: an unanchored check would have accepted teal #1baf7a as a colour, which is exactly what a paste from a design tool looks like.

And one thing got fixed on the way that had been blocking you without either of us seeing it. The Products screen decided whether you could edit a row by asking which structures you administer — which is not the question the database asks. A site whose structure is shared with another site dropped out of that list entirely, so its admin saw every button greyed out while the server would have accepted every one of their changes. It now lets you try and shows the server’s answer, which since the error work two stages back is a plain sentence rather than a shrug.

And then you told me a fourth time, and this time I rendered the screen instead of reading the code. Here is what was on it. Every product row offered three buttons — Deactivate, Rename, Delete — and the “Belongs to” column was plain text. There was no button called Edit anywhere on the row. The picker you asked for, the code box and the name box were all behind the button marked Rename, and “rename” promises that only the name changes. You read that row, correctly concluded a product could not be moved, and told me so. Press it and everything works — the dropdown is there, the whole tree is in it, and Save sends the new place. The feature was finished three sessions ago. The door was labelled wrong. The same button on shift patterns had the same problem. Both now say Edit, and hovering says what is behind them: change its code, its name, or where it belongs. Operators already said Edit — which is exactly why you stopped reporting the operator half and kept reporting this one.

Why every test was green while you were blocked, which is worth more than the fix. The check I wrote last session to make this impossible reads three files of database code and asks whether the word for “where it belongs” still appears near the word for “change it”. It would have passed with the entire Products screen deleted from the project. Underneath that: nothing in this project had ever tested a screen — 24 test files, all of them testing the machinery behind the screens, none of them looking at one. There is now a first one, and the thing that makes it different is that it hunts for buttons by the words printed on them, the way you do. I broke the code seven ways on purpose; it caught all seven, and correctly ignored a harmless eighth.

One more thing the picture showed. None of the five demo products is attached to a plant, and a company-wide product can only be changed by a company admin — so if you sign in as one of the two site administrators, every row on that screen is greyed out, four times over. Nothing is wrong with the code there; the demo data just makes a working screen look dead. That was already on the list as a small chore and it has been moved up.

Done — you can edit a product. The database half was in and tested last session (24 new database checks, 332 to 356; 15 deliberate breakages of the database change and 11 of the screen code, none uncaught). The screen half is now genuinely reachable, and it has the project’s first test that looks at a screen: 7 new checks, 7 deliberate breakages, all caught. Run the suite and check it says 25 files, not 24 — if it still says 24, the new test did not run and the number means nothing.

§19.66 · §19.67 · D103 · D105 · D106 · migration 0025

20

“Certain people can only work in certain areas”

Your correction, and it was the right one. I had asked whether a person’s area should just filter the list, and argued that their trainings already answer where they can work. You said no — and the distinction is real: training answers “are they capable”, area answers “are they cleared to be there at all”. Both have to be true, and only one of them is about competence.

You chose the strong version: the database refuses an assignment outside someone’s area, and a supervisor can override it while recording a reason. That machinery already exists — every assignment row has carried an override flag, a reason and who made it since the first week — so this is using a pattern rather than inventing one.

★★ HALF OF THIS WAS ALREADY LIVE BEFORE THE STAGE STARTED, AND IT ARRIVED SIDEWAYS. When you replaced the model in stage 21 — “no product or operator can be assigned where it does not belong” — that is this rule, and the database has been enforcing it since that change went in. A person belongs to a place; an assignment outside it is refused outright. Nobody built it as stage 20; it fell out of the ownership rule.

★★ But what had shipped was STRICTER than what you asked for, and that was the part left to build. You asked for a refusal a supervisor can override while recording a reason. There was no override at all. Measured on a real database before this stage was built: the company admin — the one account no permission check refuses — asking with the override flag set and a reason attached, on a cell in their own company:

The override flag that already existed governed the training check and nothing else, so it did not reach this rule at all. That is the same trap the note below warns about, arriving from the other direction: I was watching for the weak permission accidentally granting the strong one, and what actually happened is that the strong one shipped with no permission attached to it whatsoever.

★ Your call, and it is what got built: anyone who can schedule there. A supervisor with edit rights on that cell can place someone outside their area, recording a reason — so the area rule is a strong warning rather than a wall, and the record carries who waved it through and what they said.

★★ The door is a COLUMN on the row, not an argument to a form, and that is the whole design. The refusal is a database rule that fires on any write — including a plain edit that passes through no form at all. An override plumbed through the assignment form would have worked on one screen and refused on the next, which is exactly the failure the note above warned about. Putting the flag and the reason on the assignment itself means every way of writing one carries it: the form, dragging a run to another cell, and splitting coverage — three layers, and splitting coverage is the middle one that quietly drops things.

★ Two flags and two reasons, deliberately. Waving through “no Welding ticket” and waving through “not cleared for this plant” are now separate decisions with separate reasons, because one is about competence and the other is about permission, and one supervisor saying yes to the first must not silently say yes to the second.

★ The flag also cannot be used to claim credit for nothing. If the assignment did not actually need an override, the database turns the flag back off and clears the reason before storing it. Otherwise “this was overridden” would come to mean “somebody sent a flag”, and the record would fill with waved-through placements that never needed waving through.

★★ And dragging a run now names the people, instead of failing on whichever row it hit first. A run moved to another cell may carry a crew of six, two of whom are outside their area. The old behaviour was to stop at the first one and report that single row; the move now checks the whole crew up front and tells you every name that would need waving through, so you decide once rather than six times.

★ The PRODUCT half deliberately has no door, and that is load-bearing. A person is cleared or not cleared — a human judgement a supervisor can make. A product belonging somewhere is a fact about the structure, and stage 21’s delete-that-keeps-the-past depends on it being absolute: the written proof that deleting a part can be done safely by an ordinary admin rests on the product rule having no way through. So one half got an override and the other half was left alone on purpose. On screen they are treated oppositely too: a product outside its scope is removed from the picker — offering something that will certainly be refused is the same defect as a button named after less than it does — while a person outside theirs stays in the list with a note, because you are allowed to place them.

Done, applied and green. One database change carrying the flag, the reason, all three write paths and the up-front crew check. Twenty-three new checks; 468 database checks pass here, up from 445. Broken fifteen ways on purpose: thirteen caught, and the other two written down — one is the harmless control, the other a branch that a rule one line later makes unreachable, kept because it costs nothing. Twice during that run the instrument itself was wrong, and the second time is the one worth reading — see the findings below. On screen: the override block on the assignment form mirrors the training override you already know, and the crew names come through on a refused drag. ⚠ One screen has not caught up — the Operators admin panel still answers “where can this person work” on trainings alone and ignores their area entirely. You found it; it is the first finding below and the next thing to build. That screen caught up in session 18 (§19.77): three states, and the list cut at the person's own plant.

as the COMPANY ADMIN, override := true, reason := 'supervisor says it is fine' --> REFUSED PT409 "That person does not belong to this part of the structure."

§19.75 · §19.76 · §19.77 · D113 · migration 0030

21

“No member from one plant should see info for other plants”

Your words, and they overturn a decision I made three days ago. When the shared lists (products, people, trainings, shift patterns) got an owning plant, I made ownership decide who may change a row and deliberately left reading open to everyone in the company. I had a reason and I wrote it down. The reason was about how one piece of code works, not about how the business works, and your frame was always about reading: each site is its own instance of the app.

The rule, in one line. You can see a row if it is marked company-wide, or if the plant that owns it and a place you have been given are on the same branch — either direction. A supervisor on Line 1 sees Plant 1’s products, because Line 1 is inside Plant 1. Plant 2 does not, because those two branches never meet. Company administrators still see everything. Company-wide rows stay visible to everyone, which is what “we can have defaults” meant.

Why it is not a one-line change, and this is the honest part. Three things have to move in order. First, the check that decides whether a person is qualified for a job currently looks up their trainings as that person — so the moment they cannot see a training, they silently stop being qualified for it and the app refuses to assign them, with no error explaining why. That has to be fixed before anything is hidden, not after. Second, the board has to keep showing products that already appear on the schedule even if they belong elsewhere, or your history fills up with “unknown product” in a single colour. Third — and I only found this while looking — the board was hardcoded to show Plant 1 to everybody. Signing in as Plant 2 asked the server for Plant 1’s schedule; you saw almost nothing because the runs themselves were already protected, but the main screen of the app was pointed at the wrong plant for anyone outside Plant 1. That was a worse bug than the one you reported, and it is fixed below.

One more thing that was supposed to exist and does not. When we agreed that “belongs to” would decide where something is offered — a product on Line 1 appearing on Line 1’s cells and nowhere else — I built the picker and the rule, wrote tests for the rule, and never connected it to the board. It has been sitting there unused. It gets connected here.

Built and checked, the database half. Signed in as Quinn on a real database, before: four products (one of them Plant 1’s), nine people, two shift patterns. After: only her own and the company-wide ones. Exactly two things in the suite broke and both were alarms I had set for this change. There is a new test file of eighteen checks, and I broke the change eleven different ways on purpose to confirm they catch it — ten caught, and the eleventh was a harmless edit that correctly caught nothing.

★ It also found something worse. The check that decides whether someone is qualified was reading their trainings as that person, so a requirement set on a whole plant was invisible to a supervisor with one department. Same cell, same person: an administrator was told “not qualified” and a supervisor was told “qualified”. Fixed by the same change.

And the board half, which was the visible part. It now asks for the place YOU can see, names it in the header, and offers a chooser when you can see more than one. The obvious version would have given every supervisor an empty board — asking “which plants can you see” returns nothing for someone whose access is one department. Measured before writing it, then written the other way round: the highest thing you can see, whatever level that is.

★ You reopened this, and you were right. Your words: “plant 1 admin can see products from other site, while plant 2 admin can only see their own and company-wide ones.” Same code, two different answers — so it was never about who you are, and I went and reproduced it on the real database before touching anything.

What was actually happening. The rule I built has a second door in it, on purpose: a product stays readable if it is already sitting on a run you can see. That door exists so your schedule history doesn’t fill up with grey “unknown product” blocks. But the products list was walking through the same door. Dana administers Plant 1; a Plant 2 product happened to be scheduled on one of Plant 1’s cells, so it appeared in her catalogue. Quinn saw nothing extra only because no Plant 1 product happened to be scheduled inside Plant 2. The difference between the two of them was luck about what was on the board, not anything about their access — which is exactly why one looked correct and the other did not.

Fixed for products. The catalogue now lists only what you own or what is company-wide, full stop, and where a foreign-owned product is on your schedule it says so in one line rather than hiding it silently: “1 product scheduled here belongs to another site, so it isn’t listed.” Six new checks, five deliberate breakages, four caught and one measured harmless. I rendered the screen and looked at it: the Plant 2 product is gone and the line is there.

Not yet fixed for people and shift patterns, and that is why this stage is open again. Operators go through the same second door, and there it is worse — an operator from another plant assigned to one of your cells shows up in your people list with no indication at all. Shift patterns have the same shape. Both got the same treatment — but not the one planned here; read on.

★★ And then you changed the model instead of the code, which was the right call. Your words: “remove company-wide as an option for products and operators. Each site has its own set… no product or operator can be assigned where it does not belong.” Here is why that is a better fix than the one I was about to make. The leak existed because a Plant 2 product could be scheduled on a Plant 1 cell, and I had to keep those readable so your history didn’t fill with grey blocks. If a product can only ever be scheduled inside the site that owns it, that situation cannot arise at all. So the exception got deleted rather than patched a third time — and the same door closed for people and shift patterns at the same moment, instead of needing two more fixes and one more for every screen built after them.

What is built and checked. Every product, person, training and shift pattern now belongs to something — and to any level, so a product can belong to Line 1 and be offered on Line 1 and nowhere else. The rule is one sentence with no “except”: you see a row if the place that owns it and a place you have been given are on the same branch. Three more things turned up while doing it: an owner could be moved out from under its own history (re-home a product to another plant and every run it already had became a foreign row on your board — the same bug through a door no insert guards); two tables were still readable company-wide and nobody had looked, so a Plant 2 supervisor could list which trainings Plant 1’s cells require; and a person could hold another plant’s training. All three closed.

★ And the database change shipped with a real bug that only one kind of test could see. The step that gives existing rows an owner used a Postgres function that does not exist. It never runs on a fresh database — there is nothing to convert — so all 403 checks passed and it would have failed on the first real customer. The upgrade test caught it on its first run. That test exists because of a rule written down three weeks ago; this is the day it paid for itself.

★ And the data is new too, which is what you were asking about. The old Plant 1 with Maria and Widget X is the test fixture — about eighteen checks rest on that plant being the only one, so it stays exactly as it is and the app stopped using it. What the app shows now is Plant A, Plant B and Plant C, each with two areas, three lines and six cells, its own part numbers, its own people, its own trainings and its own shift pattern. And deliberately not everything belongs to a whole plant — each site has one part that belongs to a single line and one to a single area, because a world where everything belongs to a plant cannot show you the rule working at all.

★★ Sign in as Ana. She is a supervisor with access to one line, and she is the one person in the cast who can show you both halves of the rule at once: she sees the two parts that belong to the whole of Plant A, because they sit above her; she sees the part that belongs to her own line; and she does not see the one that belongs to Area 2, because that branch and hers never meet. Her board opens on her line rather than on the plant. Measured on a real database, not predicted.

★★ The delete warning is built, and the rule is yours in one line: “the row disappears from the list and from anything not yet started; completed runs keep their record of it.” So deleting a part removes the jobs booked for next week along with the people rostered on them, and leaves last month’s jobs on the board still saying which part they made — the code, the name and the colour are copied onto them at the moment the part goes. Same for a person: their finished shifts keep their name on them.

★ “Not yet started” is decided by the CLOCK, and that mattered more than it sounds. Every job in the system carries a status field, and the obvious reading is “delete the ones marked planned”. Nothing in the app has ever moved that field — every job ever created is still marked planned, including the ones that finished last month — so that version would have deleted the history instead of keeping it. It goes by the start time instead. And a job with no start time at all is kept: a row whose start we cannot name is not one to delete.

The dialog asks the server before it asks you. It names real counts — “1 job on the schedule, 2 shifts for people” — and both dangerous buttons stay dead until that answer arrives, because a confirmation you can click through before it knows what it is confirming is worse than none, since it looks like one. The confirm button carries the count in its own label rather than saying “Delete for good?”, which is the same rule as the button that said “Rename” and did three things.

★ “Deactivate instead” is only pushed when something is actually at risk, and that is a decision rather than a detail. Deleting a part nobody has ever scheduled destroys nothing, and a warning that fires when there is nothing to warn about is one people learn to click past — after which the one that matters gets clicked past too. Three levels, not two: nothing at stake, only finished work at stake, or work not yet started at stake. Only the third pushes you towards deactivating.

★ Deleting a training now takes it off everyone who holds it — and that is what makes it possible at all. A training can belong to one line while a plant-wide person legitimately holds it, and the admin who owns that line has no rights over that person’s record. Without the change, “delete this training” would be a dead end nobody in the company could finish. The dialog counts the people first.

★★ And the whole delete runs as YOU, with no elevated privileges — which is the rule you set last time paying for itself. Deleting a part reaches jobs on cells you never named. A week ago that would have needed the operation to run as somebody bigger than you, with its own permission story to get wrong. Because nothing can now be scheduled outside the place that owns it, anything the delete can reach is already inside your own authority — there is a written proof of that in the change itself, and a check that stops the operation dead if it ever stops being true.

Two things the app was saying that had quietly become false. A refusal message still offered “company-wide products can only be changed by a company admin” — a day after company-wide products stopped existing. It compiled, and it had a passing test that checked for that exact wrong sentence. And a rule on the People screen refused to delete anyone still holding a training, telling you to “remove them first” — work that, after this change, no longer needs doing. A stale permission check fails loudly the first time somebody tries it; a stale refusal never fails at all — it just quietly stops people doing something they are allowed to do. Both deleted rather than reworded.

One thing deliberately left half-done, and named so it is not a surprise. Trainings and shift patterns now have an on/off switch in the database, so all four lists mean the same thing — but no screen shows or sets it yet, so the dialog offers no “deactivate instead” for those two. Building the switch without the screen would be the “add form, no edit form” mistake again; it is written down as owed instead.

What is left in this stage

  • Read scoping — one plant can no longer see another's rows.
  • Ownership on every shared list, at any level, not just whole plants.
  • The board's picker and its crew list both follow it.
  • The naming rule that blocked the starter set — two plants can each have a “Forklift” now.
  • The starter set — DONE (Sep 1), and it changed shape. Rather than a curated catalogue every plant copies parts out of, a new plant is created as a copy of an existing plant’s structure — the whole node tree, atomically — and then filled in by import. Your redefinition, and it is the last thing stage 21 was holding.

In progress. Two database changes, twenty-eight new checks between them, and seventeen deliberate breakages to confirm the checks catch what they claim; a third database change on top of them, with eighteen more checks and ten more deliberate breakages — nine caught and the tenth a harmless edit that correctly caught nothing. 445 database checks pass here, up from 408, and the delete change was broken sixteen ways on purpose: fifteen caught, one a harmless edit that correctly caught nothing. One of the sixteen came back uncaught on the first run and was a real hole — taking the permission check out of the delete left every check passing, because a second gate underneath refuses it anyway and produces the same error code; what it changes is the sentence you would read, from “you may not administer this site” to “the product could not be deleted”, which is not something anyone can act on. A check now covers that. You ran it: 1118 app tests in 27 files, and two went red on the first pass. Both were an audit of mine doing exactly its job — a new stylesheet under the admin folder that I had not added to the list that audit walks. Fixed, and then measured on your machine rather than predicted: the list is complete, removing the new entry makes the audit name it again so the entry is not decorative, and the new stylesheet has no unscaled sizes in it. All of it has since landed — read scoping (migrations 0026/0027, sessions 15–17), ownership at any level (0028, session 16), the board's picker and crew list (0027/0030, session 17), the naming rule (0031, session 18) and the starter set (0035, session 19, 1 Sept) — so the stage closed in session 19.

§19.68 · §19.69 · §19.70 · §19.71 · §19.72 · §19.72a · §19.73 · §19.74 · §19.80 · §19.83 · D107 · D108 · D109 · D110 · D111 · D111a · D112 · migrations 0026–0029, 0031, 0035

DoneMine
22

Trainings, not tickets — with a sign-off

You are right that the word is wrong, and righter that the record is thin. What exists today is a list of trainings and, per person, an expiry date. That is it. So the question an audit actually asks — who signed this person off, and when — cannot be answered at all.

So: the word changes everywhere on screen, and the record grows two fields — trained on, and signed off by a named person — alongside the expiry it already has. And trainings get their own section, so a supervisor manages the list and sees who holds what, instead of it living behind a link inside Operators.

What is left in this stage

  • Trainings have their own tab, beside Operators and Products.
  • The word changed — nothing a reader sees says “ticket” any more.
  • Retire and bring back, so a training can be withdrawn without stripping it off everyone who holds it.
  • The database side of the sign-off — who signed it off, and when.
  • Supervisors are allowed to keep the record, on their own line or plant and nowhere else.
  • The sign-off is on the screen — when it was done and who signed it, editable on every training somebody holds.
  • Supervisors are in, seeing Operators and Trainings and nothing else.
  • Controls a supervisor cannot use are not offered — a row they can see but not change says so, instead of showing buttons that get refused.
  • Nothing — stage 22 is done. Two small follow-ups turned up on the way and are noted at the top of this page rather than left buried in here.

Half of it is in, and it is the half you asked about. Trainings have their own tab, beside Operators and Products. Before this they were a “Ticket types” toggle INSIDE the Operators screen, reachable only after picking somebody — so managing the list meant choosing an arbitrary person first. The word changed everywhere a reader can see it; the database keeps its own vocabulary, which is a different thing and stays. The second half landed in session 18 (D114, migration 0032): the sign-off record with a free-text signer, supervisors keeping the record on their own branch, and controls withheld on rows a supervisor cannot change — the stage was CLOSED in session 18.

D104 · D114 · migration 0032

Delivers R-D104

DoneMine
23

CSV import

Bulk-load a hierarchy, operators and products from a spreadsheet.

What is left in this stage

  • Trainings are ready — they carry the id a spreadsheet matches on, so a second upload updates instead of duplicating.
  • Places can be matched now. The original blocker: a row in your spreadsheet could not be tied to a place in the hierarchy, so the tree could not be imported at all. Fixed — places carry an id and a note of where they came from.
  • Parts have their rule. They had the column and nothing stopping two of them claiming the same id, so a second upload would have quietly built a duplicate set.
  • Settled, and built: a part is company-wide, made in a LIST of plants (Sep 1). The decision that was yours is answered — the number is the company’s, and the plants it is made in are a list (migration 0034). So two plants can both hold SKU-9 because it is one part made in both, and an import that names overlapping part numbers now updates the shared part rather than being refused.
  • People are matched company-wide; parts belong to a list of plants. Left as noted, but an importer has to know it: one code is one company-wide part offered in several plants, and one person.
  • The upload screen — DONE for products and operators (Sep 1). Choose a file, preview what it will do to every row (add, update, or error), fix what is wrong, then apply; a second upload of the same file updates rather than duplicating. Plus a downloadable template and an in-app legend. The wizard was refactored generic — products and operators share one ImportWizard with an entity-tab picker.
  • Training import — DONE (Sep 1). The wizard now has four tabs: Products, People, Trainings (bulk-add training types per plant) and Certifications (people’s records, matched by employee reference and training name on the person’s branch, with sign-off and expiry). All four run in the browser.
  • Tree CSV import — dropped. The starter library gives a new plant its structure by copying and the visual tree editor beats a parents-and-children CSV, so it is not worth building outside a rare ERP bulk-load.

Its premise doesn't exist — and today's survey found a second one missing. Nodes have no external reference to match an imported row against, which we knew. Products have the column but no uniqueness rule on it, so the products half can't match either. And a third thing worth knowing before anyone writes this: since stage 09, creating a new top-level site quietly makes it its own copy of a structure — which is correct on a screen where you create one site at a time, and wrong for an import of twelve, where it would silently manufacture twelve structures. Needs its own database change first, and the written description of that function is out of date on top of it. Both premises were fixed since (external ids on places and a uniqueness rule on parts), and all four importers — products, people, trainings and certifications — shipped on 1 Sept (session 19) with the tree importer dropped by agreement, so the stage closed by session 20.

25

One plant filter for the whole admin screen

One control on the admin page, read by every section, with no panel gaining a prop. The choice is remembered across visits and the chosen plant is spelled out in the header of every tab; one readable root means no control and no header row at all; and the filter narrows the forms too, so what you see is what you can create in. It compares the hierarchy path, not the parent id, because the Shifts panel throws the parent away when it reshapes its nodes. The Hierarchy tab is filtered on its rows and only its rows, because the tree editor must keep the complete node array or it starts refusing moves the server would accept.

Four real defects fell out of the work, none of them the feature — three of one shape (a dropdown whose value is not among its options renders its first option and reports nothing, so control and write disagree) and a screen test that had been green through the wrong branch for its whole life.

Shipped in session 18 (§19.79). Built without a stage card at the time; this card was added on 2 Sept when the plan file was assembled. Still owed from it: the canEdit defect — all three scopeOptions callers pass none — kept out deliberately because it is a permission and the filter is a view choice.

§19.79 · roadmap 1(c)

Delivers R-280, R-281, R-282, R-283, R-284, R-285, R-286

DoneMine
26

The status columns that never meant anything are gone

Three columns that looked like features and were not. An assignment carried a progress label nothing read, a "deleted" assignment was only marked, and a run carried a status that one overlap rule had to make an exception for. Each was a word in the schema that a screen showed and no rule obeyed, which is worse than an absent feature: it reads as a promise.

Deleting an assignment now deletes it. Assignments carry no status at all, runs carry none either, and the overlap rule stops carving out the one value that used to be exempt — so the constraint says what it means for every row rather than for most of them.

Shipped in sessions 53 and 54. Built without a stage card at the time; this card was added on 5 Sept in the buildout catch-up, on the same footing as S25's.

DoneMine
27

Standard cycle times, and the level above that adds up — or refuses to

How long a part takes at a place, set per cell per product in seconds, on a grid shaped like the hierarchy rather than repeated inside every product row. An assignment with no typed target shows what its cell's cycle time implies, so the number a planner did not enter is still visible as the one the cell would produce.

⛔ AND THE ROLL-UP LEARNED THAT ADDING UP IS NOT ALWAYS RIGHT. R-317 summed every measured place below a row on the reading that a sum is the labour content of one unit. That holds for two cells in a line — sequential stations, so a unit really does cost 60 s + 90 s — and is nonsense for two lines under an area, which are ALTERNATIVE ROUTES no unit ever takes both of. Migration 0041 gives a node sums_children, nullable, so unset RESOLVES on read rather than the column guessing. The stage is worth reading for that correction rather than for the feature.

Shipped in sessions 51 and 52, the roll-up correction (R-319) with it. Built without a stage card at the time; this card was added on 5 Sept in the buildout catch-up.

28

One shell, one field, one chevron — the screens stop each inventing their own

The same thing built three times is three things. There were three parallel floating-panel implementations — the board's, a line-for-line clone on admin, and a hand-rolled one that had no focus trap and no Escape. The field box was hand-copied into eleven stylesheets, so the twelfth screen had nothing to import and silently got the browser's own. Four affordances each drew their own chevron, which is how the maintainer noticed: it looked different on the board than on admin.

Each is now ONE module with a build audit forbidding a second — Popover.tsx, Field.module.css / InlineEdit.tsx, and icons.tsx. The audits are the deliverable, not the consolidation: a standard nothing enforces is a convention, and a convention is what produced eleven copies.

⚠️ AND ONE OF THEM REACHED THE DATABASE. The shared pop-up stopped NEW assignments getting a stray target_unit, but a code change only decides the next write. The maintainer: "a rule is a rule, be it new or old." Migration 0039 backfills the old rows and adds the CHECK, so the invariant is a fact of the schema rather than something the UI remembers.

Shipped across sessions 50-52. Built without a stage card at the time; this card was added on 5 Sept in the buildout catch-up.

Edit is one door, on both screens that have one

Edit opens everything editable about the row, and Cancel closes all of it. A shift pattern's Edit reaches its name, its owner, and its shifts and breaks together, rather than being a rename that leaves the times behind a second click. A product's Edit reaches its code, its name and its colour, and the colour is STAGED until Save like everything else in the panel.

Both reverse an earlier narrow-Edit scoping at the maintainer's request, and the reason is the same each time: the row had two controls disagreeing about it. The pattern used to expand its shifts on its own, which left the Edit button reading "Edit" over an already-open row.

The plant you chose reaches past the admin screen

DoneMine
31

The Activity log: who changed what, and when

The audit log had been written by trigger since 0008 and never read. This is the screen: what changed, where, and when, attributed to a person, with a filter that says in words what it searched rather than leaving the reader to infer it from what came back.

⚠️ IT IS THE STRICTEST GATE IN THE APP AND THAT IS DELIBERATE. audit_log_select is app_is_admin() and org_id = app_current_org() — the ORG-WIDE role, so a site admin gets ZERO ROWS rather than a refusal, and an empty list reads exactly like "nothing has ever changed here". The tab is therefore hidden from them by companyAdminOnly, which is the only tab that flag is for. DEF-0007 is what happened when it was carried by a second one.

It is also the first table in the app to meet PostgREST's thousand-row ceiling, so it pages.

A setting is answered for a plant, not only for the company

The company had exactly one write RPC for its settings and no screen to change most of them. This stage is the Settings tab: the date-display format first (R-309), then the shape that lets any setting be answered for ONE PLANT and fall back to the company when it is not (0050, 0052).

Delivers R-309, R-320, R-331, R-332, R-333, R-335

DoneMine
33

Things that can be brought to an end

Two ways for something to stop being current, which the app had no way to say. A shift pattern can be retired and brought back, and retiring is careful about what it does NOT do: it detaches nothing, changes no board, and a place already running a retired pattern still shows it in its own picker. A retired pattern that places still run says so and counts them.

⚠️ THE RETIRE FLAG IS ADVISORY AND THE CLIENT IS THE WHOLE OF THE RULE — 0029's own column comment says so, resolve_shift_template never reads it, board_window does not emit it. Worth knowing before anyone builds on it.

A certification, by contrast, ends on the SERVER: it cannot outlive the plant it was earned in (migration 0045), because a training is a fact about a person at a place and the place can go.

Shipped in sessions 59 and 60. Built without a stage card at the time; this card was added on 5 Sept in the buildout catch-up.

Operator training matrix

The operator training matrix — its own buildout

Who on the team is trained on what, at a glance — a colour grid a supervisor reads without opening anyone's record. A new view over data the app already loads: no migration, no server function, no new read.

5 stages shipped — open to read them
1

The pure core

A dependency-free module a supervisor never sees, and the whole feature rests on it. Everything load-bearing decided from plain values — no React, no Supabase — the way scope.ts and operators.ts already are, so it is unit-tested under vitest with no browser. It computes: the nested column header (each training's owner walked up to the common root, grouped into bands with the right colspans and rowspans, own-trainings of a node bucketed as “… area-wide” beside its children); the cell state for an operator×training from the holding and its expiry against a “today” and a window; applicability / N/A via isAtOrBelow; scope filtering of rows and columns; and team grouping of the rows.

What is in this stage

  • Cell-state derivation with the expiry boundaries pinned (expired when expires_at < today; expiring inside the window; a null expiry never expires).
  • The nested-header builder, general to any depth, with the ordering rule (own-before-children, ancestors merged) and the rowspan for shallow buckets.
  • Scope + applicability on ltree paths, mirroring the server so no shown tick can be a lie.
  • A full vitest suite — the expiry edges, the header shapes (single plant, company root, an area scope pulled up to its plant by a plant-wide training), N/A, ordering, grouping.

The Matrix tab

A new admin section beside Operators / Trainings, switched on by its own MATRIX_PANEL_READY flag (the pre-seat rule — a section can't appear without a panel behind it). It reads the existing useOperatorsAdmin data, renders the grid from the core, and carries the hierarchy cascade (plant › area › line, each defaulting to All, rooted at what the viewer can read) and the operator multi-select. The plant filter that already lives on AdminPage becomes the top of that cascade.

Defects
