DEF-0001 · major · doc-drift · verified

The committed e2e smoke test still expects the signed-out Board, so CI is red the first time it runs

Violates R-321 · filed 2026-09-02 by tester · fix 3e6e2ed

Reproduction

Run the smoke test against a clean dummy-backend server — the exact state ci.yml uses (VITE_SUPABASE_URL=https://example.supabase.co), so no session and no data:

``` # terminal 1 — a throwaway dev server with dummy Supabase values, on a spare port VITE_SUPABASE_URL=https://example.supabase.co VITE_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY=dummy-anon-key \ npx vite --port 5174 --strictPort

# terminal 2 — the repo's own e2e, pointed at that server npx playwright test --config <a config whose baseURL is http://localhost:5174 and whose webServer block is removed> ```

Equivalently, and more simply: this is exactly what npm run e2e does inside .github/workflows/ci.yml (job build-and-test, step npm run e2e). That workflow triggered on [main] for the repository's first 43 commits while the default branch was Development, so it never ran; commit 21c868f widened the trigger to branches: [main, Development] plus pull_request, so the next push is the first time CI has ever executed this step.

Expected

R-321 (filed as R-304, renumbered on merge): "a signed-out visitor to any route other than /sign-in is redirected to /sign-in ... The committed e2e smoke test (e2e/smoke.spec.ts) must therefore pin the app as it is — the sign-in redirect — and npm run e2e must be green on main." The app already behaves this way: src/routes.tsx wraps the whole shell in <RequireAuth />, which redirects a sessionless visitor to /sign-in (src/features/auth/RequireAuth.tsx:39-45). The product is correct; the test is what is stale.

Actual

All three smoke cases fail. Against the clean dummy-backend server on 5174 the signed-out visitor is served the sign-in screen, not the Board:

``` 1) [chromium] › e2e\smoke.spec.ts:35:1 › D97: a visitor with no session is offered no Admin link Error: expect(locator).toBeVisible() failed Locator: getByRole('heading', { name: 'Board', level: 1 }) Expected: visible Timeout: 5000ms Error: element(s) not found

3 failed [chromium] › e2e\smoke.spec.ts:28:1 › the shell and the board render without a backend [chromium] › e2e\smoke.spec.ts:35:1 › D97: a visitor with no session is offered no Admin link [chromium] › e2e\smoke.spec.ts:48:1 › D97: /admin visited directly refuses instead of rendering ```

The page snapshot Playwright captured at the moment of failure is the sign-in screen, not the signed-out shell:

```yaml

  • heading "Production Scheduler" [level=1]
  • paragraph: Sign in to continue.
  • text: Email
  • textbox "Email"
  • text: Password
  • textbox "Password"
  • button "Sign in"
  • text: "Dev sign-in (local only): Viewing as:"
  • combobox "Viewing as:":

- option "Signed out" [selected] ```

Case 3 (/admin while signed out) fails the same way: it waits for a "Not available" heading, but the sign-in gate intercepts the visit before RequireAdmin is ever reached, so the D97 refusal screen the test asserts is never rendered for a signed-out actor.

Lead

The app is right; the test is a week behind it. e2e/smoke.spec.ts was deliberately rewritten in f2ac33d around a signed-out Board shell — its header even reasons that "useSession calls loadProfile(null) ... so adminAccess settles on 'denied'" and the Board renders. P1-6b (session 19, Sep 1) then wrapped the whole shell in RequireAuth and removed that shell: there is no signed-out Board any more, only a redirect to /sign-in. The test warns about exactly this failure mode in its own comment ("A test that has never executed is not a test — it is a file") and has now reproduced it a second time.

The developer's call, but the shape of a fix: rewrite the three cases to pin the app as P1-6b left it — / while signed out shows heading "Production Scheduler" and paragraph "Sign in to continue" and no Admin link; /admin while signed out lands on /sign-in, not "Not available". Note that once the signed-out cases assert the sign-in redirect, D97's genuine admin refusal ("Not available" for a signed-in non-admin) is left with no e2e that reaches it — R-D97 is still verified_by: []. This is a stale-test / broken-CI defect, not a product bug; no vitest pin is written because the reproduction is a browser run, given as the command above.

Re-verified 2026-09-04 (tester)

Green at 3e6e2ed. My reproduction was npm run e2e against the app; the run is now clean:

`` ok playwright e2e ``

The three cases were rewritten to pin the sign-in redirect rather than the signed-out Board shell P1-6b removed, which is what the defect asked for. No pin existed, so there is nothing to promote. R-321 now carries an e2e verifier.

DEF-0002 · major · stale-permission · verified

On Plant A's board, the New popover offers parts that are only made in Plant B and Plant C, and the server refuses every one of them

Violates R-272, R-239 · filed 2026-09-04 by tester · fix 30aff5f · pinned by src/test/scope.test.ts

Reproduction

In the running app at http://localhost:5174 (a dev server on the committed tip b32ead3; http://localhost:5173 does the same):

1. Sign in with the dev switcher as Admin (company — all three plants). 2. On the Board, leave the plant picker on Plant A. 3. Drag across Cell 1's lane to open the New popover. 4. Open the Product dropdown. 5. Choose Housing B and press Create.

Expected

R-272: "The create popover offers only products that belong at the cell and are active." Housing B is made at Plant B. Plant A's Cycle times grid agrees — its columns are PN-1001, PN-1002, PN-1003, PN-1004 and PN-9001 (Common Fastener), and no Plant B part appears — so the same question, asked on the admin screen, gives the right answer. The board should offer the same five.

R-239: "A client control is never ... shown for something the server refuses."

Actual

The dropdown offers twelve parts, eight of which belong to other plants (read from the live control):

`` ["Housing A","Bracket A","Line 1 Subassembly A","Housing B","Bracket B", "Line 1 Subassembly B","Area 2 Frame B","Housing C","Bracket C", "Line 1 Subassembly C","Area 2 Frame C","Common Fastener"] ``

Choosing Housing B and pressing Create is refused by the database. Nothing is created. The response body, captured off the wire:

`` HTTP 409 v1/rpc/create_run {"code":"PT409", "details":"{\"id\": \"b30f1677-e8b4-4465-bf3d-58caf2c9c4b0\", \"kind\": \"product\", \"error\": \"not_offered_here\", \"node_id\": \"ce0d946d-d104-4c2e-be48-869220e3bad4\"}", "hint":null, "message":"That product does not belong to this part of the structure."} ``

Note that "Area 2 Frame A" is correctly ABSENT from the list — it is a Plant A part scoped to Area 2, and Cell 1 is in Area 1. So the scope test is running and is right about the parts it can see; it is only wrong about the parts belonging to a plant that is not on screen.

The pin src/test/defects/DEF-0002.test.ts is red on this build:

`` FAIL src/test/defects/DEF-0002.test.ts > DEF-0002: the board's product picker is scoped to the plant it is showing > does NOT offer a part made only at another plant ``

Lead

A suspicion, not a fact.

BoardPage.tsx:290-304 builds the offered list with productsOfferedHere(active, path, index.nodeById). index.nodeById holds only the selected root's subtree — board_window scopes it, and the file says so itself twenty lines further down, in operatorPool's comment:

> "index.nodeById IS exactly this plant's subtree ... An earlier version > resolved owner PATHS through nodeById and 'failed open' on an owner it could > not find — but a different plant's owner is never in the scoped map, so every > out-of-plant operator was kept: the bug this replaces."

productOfferedAt (scope.ts) fails open per place for the same reason the old operator code did — if (place === undefined) return true. A Plant B part's place is a Plant B node, which is never in a Plant A board's map, so the fail-open fires on every one of them. R-310 fixed exactly this for operators in session 32 by switching to membership (ownedInScope); the product half was left on the path compare.

The fail-open itself is not wrong where it was designed for — a scope node the reader may not READ. What is wrong is asking it against a map that was narrowed by a VIEW CHOICE rather than by permission. scope.ts's own header warns about this confusion in another context ("NOT TO BE CONFUSED WITH THE PLANT FILTER (§19.79) ... That is a VIEW CHOICE the reader made and can undo; this was a PERMISSION"). The developer decides, but the shape that suggests itself is the one operatorPool already uses: resolve places against membership in index.nodeById, and treat a place outside the scoped set as a real "not this plant" rather than an "I cannot tell".

Note the two halves of the design that this breaks. CreatePopover.tsx:41-47 records the decision that a product outside its scope is filtered OUT precisely because "offering the product would offer a guaranteed refusal" — which is now what happens. And the refusal it leans on does not arrive either; see DEF-0003.

Re-verified 2026-09-04 (tester)

Green at 30aff5f, both halves of my reproduction.

The pin passes cold, and the click path gives the right list. As the company admin on Plant A / Cell 1 the picker now reads:

`` ["Housing A","Bracket A","Line 1 Subassembly A","Common Fastener"] ``

four entries instead of twelve, with the eight other-plant parts gone and "Area 2 Frame A" still correctly absent (a Plant A part scoped to Area 2).

I also mutated the fix back — if (place === undefined) return true — and ran the suite. Five named cases go red (scope.test.ts XP4, XP6, XP8, XP9 and this defect's pin), so the behaviour is genuinely held, not incidentally green.

The pin is still under src/test/defects/; the developer may promote it into scope.test.ts beside XP6, which now covers the same ground.

⚠️ One thing this fix did NOT reach, filed separately as DEF-0005: the picker is now correct for a reader whose board root is a plant, and offers a supervisor whose root is a LINE only one of the four parts the server accepts. Case XP9 asserts that absence is correct; it is not. That is a pre-existing fault, not a regression from this commit — [].some(...) was false before it too.

Developer note

⭐ THE PIN WAS PROMOTED IN 808a52d, as CLAUDE.md allows once a defect is verified: the copy under src/test/defects/ is deleted and its claim lives on as case XP4 in src/test/scope.test.ts, beside the predicate it is about. The pin field above points at its new home rather than at a file that no longer exists.

The predicate it originally tested is gone too, and for a reason recorded in DEF-0005: the client no longer decides where a part may be run at all — board_window answers it (migration 0042). XP4 therefore asserts the same outcome (a part made only at another plant is not in the dropdown) against the new mechanism, which is the claim that mattered rather than the code that used to carry it.

DEF-0003 · major · doc-drift · verified

A part refused for being in the wrong place reads as 'Something went wrong. Please try again.' — the sentence the server sent never reaches the screen

Violates R-046 · filed 2026-09-04 by tester · fix 3c58d13 · pinned by src/test/defects/DEF-0003.test.ts

Reproduction

Follow DEF-0002's five steps — the same click path produces both defects — and read the toast that appears at the bottom of the board.

Or, with no browser, run the pin:

`` npx vitest run src/test/defects/DEF-0003.test.ts ``

Expected

R-046: "Clients read the JSON error field; raw constraint violations are never part of the contract." The server raised not_offered_here through api_raise and describeSchedulerError already carries the right sentence for it — errors.ts:741 is a whole comment about naming the thing rather than the rule: "what the person did was put a Plant 2 product on a Plant 1 cell, and the only useful thing to say is which kind of row was in the wrong place and what to do instead."

Actual

The server sent a named, typed refusal:

`` HTTP 409 v1/rpc/create_run {"code":"PT409", "details":"{\"id\": \"b30f1677-e8b4-4465-bf3d-58caf2c9c4b0\", \"kind\": \"product\", \"error\": \"not_offered_here\", \"node_id\": \"ce0d946d-d104-4c2e-be48-869220e3bad4\"}", "hint":null, "message":"That product does not belong to this part of the structure."} ``

The screen showed:

`` Something went wrong. Please try again. ``

which is describeSchedulerError's Unknown branch — the message that asks somebody to repeat an action that can never succeed.

The pin is red on this build, and its operator control passes:

`` FAIL DEF-0003 > classifies the product refusal as NotOfferedHere AssertionError: expected 'Unknown' to be 'NotOfferedHere' FAIL DEF-0003 > says which kind of row was in the wrong place, not 'Something went wrong' ok DEF-0003 > still classifies the operator refusal, which kept its owner_node_id ``

Lead

A suspicion, not a fact — but the two shapes can be read side by side.

parseDetail's not_offered_here branch (src/lib/api/errors.ts:455-470) requires four keys before it will match:

`` hasStringProp(detail, "kind") && hasStringProp(detail, "id") && hasStringProp(detail, "owner_node_id") && hasStringProp(detail, "node_id") ``

Migration 0028 sent all four. Migration 0034 (D115, "a product belongs to a list of plants") stopped sending owner_node_id for the two PRODUCT guards, because a part now has many places and there is no single owner node to name:

`` 20260901000034:161 jsonb_build_object('kind', 'product', 'id', new.product_id, 'node_id', new.node_id) 20260901000034:207 jsonb_build_object('kind', 'product', 'id', new.product_id, 'node_id', new.node_id) 20260903000040:178 jsonb_build_object('kind', 'product', 'id', new.product_id, 'node_id', new.node_id) ``

while the OPERATOR guard in the same file kept it:

`` 20260901000034:187 jsonb_build_object('kind', 'operator', 'id', new.operator_id, 'owner_node_id', v_owner, 'node_id', new.node_id) ``

So the parser is not broken — it is a week behind one of the two payloads it reads, which is CLAUDE.md §4's "a column list that appears twice is a bug with a delay on it" in its exact form. tsc cannot see it: the required keys are a runtime string test, not a type.

Two things follow that are worth checking with the fix rather than after it:

  • CycleTimesPanel.tsx:428 is if (err.kind === "NotOfferedHere"). Migration

0040's guard is the only thing that raises it for that panel and it omits owner_node_id too, so that branch appears to be unreachable — a written error message that cannot be shown.

  • ownerNodeId is a field on the NotOfferedHere variant. If the product

payload can no longer supply it, the variant needs it optional rather than the server needing to invent one; describeSchedulerError's sentence does not appear to use it.

Re-verified 2026-09-04 (tester)

Green at 3c58d13. My reproduction was the pin, run cold:

`` Test Files 2 passed (2) Tests 5 passed (5) ``

All three cases pass, including the operator control that was already green — so the fix widened the parser rather than loosening it into accepting anything.

The click path in the Reproduction can no longer reach this refusal at all, because DEF-0002's fix removed the offer that produced it; the pin is now the only thing standing between this and a silent return. Worth promoting into errors.test.ts Group N when the developer promotes it.

Note the developer's own F-079: a THIRD payload shape (move_run's bulk crew refusal, {kind, node_id, operators} with no id) still falls through to Unknown. That is outside this defect and correctly left open.

DEF-0004 · minor · visual · verified

On the Cycle times grid a line that has nothing measured yet looks exactly like a line that does not make the part — both are a bare dash

Violates R-317 · filed 2026-09-04 by tester · fix dab0755

Reproduction

In the running app at http://localhost:5174 (committed tip b32ead3):

1. Sign in as Admin (company — all three plants). 2. Go to Admin → Cycle times, and read Plant A's grid. 3. Look down the PN-1003 (Line 1 Subassembly A) column at the two line rows.

Expected

R-317: "Cells with no number contribute nothing rather than a zero (unmeasured work is unknown, not free) and the row reports how many contributed."

The contributors and total counts exist for exactly this. The panel's own source says why, in the comment above CycleGridCell: "A total over 3 of 5 measured cells is not wrong, but it is not the line's full labour content either, and a reader who cannot tell the two apart will plan against the smaller number." And R-319 applies the same reasoning to the row that declines to add up: it says "not added" in words "because a bare dash here would read as 'nothing entered'."

Actual

Both line rows render the same glyph:

| Row | PN-1003 shows | What is actually true | | ------- | ------------- | -------------------------------------------------- | | Line 1 | — | the part IS made at Cell 1 and Cell 2; 0 of 2 timed | | Line 2 | — | the part is made nowhere under Line 2 |

The same pair appears in the PN-1004 column: Line 3 reads — while Cell 5 and Cell 6 beneath it are editable boxes for that part.

The 0/2 the requirement asks for is not shown. CycleTimesPanel.tsx:336-348 renders the contributors/total annotation only inside the branch where contributors > 0:

``tsx {cell.contributors === 0 ? ( <span className={styles.dash}>—</span> ) : ( <> {formatCycle(cell.seconds)} {cell.contributors < cell.total && ( <span className={styles.sumNote}> {cell.contributors}/{cell.total}</span> )} </> )} ``

so the row that has the most to report — none of them measured — reports nothing, and the row that reports 3/5 is the only one that says anything at all. The na cell above it (:297-303) draws the identical dash. Only a hover title separates them, and the na cell has no title at all — it carries aria-label="not made here", which the sum cell does not, so the two are not distinguishable to a screen reader in the other direction either.

The effect on the screen is that a supervisor scanning the line rows for work that still needs timing sees nothing to do. The per-plant counter ("6 of 22 places measured") is the only signal that any exists, and it does not say where.

Lead

A suspicion, not a fact. This is presentation only — buildCycleGrid computes contributors and total correctly, and cycleTimes.test.ts case G3 pins that it does. The green case is about the MODEL; the clause it is verifying is about the ROW, and there is no test of CycleTimesPanel at all (every other panel has one: settingsPanel, productsPanel, shiftsPanel, operatorsPanel, trainingsPanel, importPanel). So this is a green case that cannot fail for the thing it names, and it is why no pin is filed here — the reproduction is a screen, and the case that would catch it is a render test the developer owns.

No fix is prescribed. The shape that suggests itself is the one R-319 already chose for the row next to it: say it in words rather than in a glyph — "0 of 2" where the sum is empty, so the three states (not made here / made here but not timed / timed) read as three different things.

Re-verified 2026-09-04 (tester)

Green at dab0755. My reproduction was a screen, and the screen now reads correctly. The exact pair from the Reproduction, Plant A's grid, PN-1003 column:

| Row | before | now | what is true | | ------ | ------- | ------------------ | --------------------------------------------------- | | Line 1 | — | 0 of 2 measured | the part IS made at Cell 1 and Cell 2; none timed | | Line 2 | — | — | the part is made nowhere under Line 2 |

PN-1004 / Line 3 moved the same way. The four states — an editable box, a sum, "not added", and a bare dash for "not made here" — are now four different things on the screen.

No pin was filed for this one, so there is nothing to promote. cycleTimesPanel.test.tsx is new and is the render test the defect said was missing; R-317 now names it.

DEF-0005 · major · stale-refusal · verified

A supervisor is offered one part out of the four on their own board, and the server accepts all four

Violates R-239 · filed 2026-09-04 by tester · fix 808a52d

Reproduction

In the running app at http://localhost:5175 (a dev server on the committed tip 75b5aac):

1. Sign in with the dev switcher as Ana (supervisor, Plant A / Line 1 only). Her board opens on Line 1 — visible_board_roots gives her the top of what she can read, which is her grant, not the plant. 2. Read the legend across the top of the board. It shows four parts: Housing A, Bracket A, Line 1 Subassembly A, Common Fastener. 3. Drag across Cell 1's lane to open the New popover. 4. Open the Product dropdown.

Marco (supervisor, Plant B / Area 1) reproduces it identically on his own board; his grant is an area rather than a line, so it is not a property of Ana's fixture.

Expected

R-239: "A client control is never hidden for something the server permits."

CLAUDE.md §4 puts the same rule the other way round: "Whatever a client hides or offers must be decided by the same test the server runs (isAtOrBelow on the path, the same predicate as the RLS policy)."

The server's test is app_product_offered_at, and asked over Ana's own session about her own Cell 1 it says yes to all four:

`` SERVER app_product_offered_at(product, plant_a.area_1.line_1.cell_1), as Ana: PN-1001 (Housing A): HTTP 200 -> true PN-1002 (Bracket A): HTTP 200 -> true PN-1003 (Line 1 Subassembly A): HTTP 200 -> true PN-9001 (Common Fastener): HTTP 200 -> true ``

Actual

The dropdown offers one:

`` select#cp-prod: ["Line 1 Subassembly A"] ``

The whole popover, read off the screen:

`` New Product run | Direct assignment Product Line 1 Subassembly A Planned headcount Fri 04/09 14:00 - 17:30 Cancel Create ``

So three of the four parts on her own legend cannot be scheduled on her own line, and nothing on the screen says why — the picker simply does not list them.

Marco's board is the same shape:

`` PN-2001 places=[] server says PN-2001=true PN-2002 places=[] server says PN-2002=true PN-2003 places=["08457f2d-..."] server says PN-2003=true PN-9001 places=[] server says PN-9001=true ``

Lead

A suspicion, not a fact.

The place list arrives EMPTY, and an empty list is read as "made nowhere". This is board_window's payload for Ana, captured off the wire:

`` board_window nodes: ["plant_a.area_1.line_1", "plant_a.area_1.line_1.cell_1", "plant_a.area_1.line_1.cell_2"] PN-1001 site_node_ids=[] PN-1002 site_node_ids=[] PN-1003 site_node_ids=["d3555515-..."] <- scoped to Line 1, which she can read PN-9001 site_node_ids=[] ``

board_window is SECURITY INVOKER, so its product_sites sub-select runs under product_sites_select (0034 §8): org_id = app_current_org() and app_can_read_node(node_id). Reading is downward from a grant, so a supervisor granted a LINE cannot read the PLANT, and a plant-wide part's only place row is dropped before it reaches the client. productOfferedAt then answers false, because [].some(...) is false — and that is the RIGHT answer for the empty list it was handed. The fault is upstream: two different states arrive as the same empty array — "this part is assigned to no plant" (the honest zero history.ts leans on) and "this part's places are all above your grant".

⚠️ THIS IS NOT A REGRESSION FROM 30aff5f. Before DEF-0002's fix the line read if (place === undefined) return true, but that branch is never reached for an empty array — some over [] short-circuits to false either way. The behaviour is identical before and after, and dates to migration 0034, when the always-present products.site_node_id COLUMN became an RLS-filtered product_sites LIST that can come back empty.

⚠️⚠️ BUT THE FIX'S OWN PROOF SAYS THIS CANNOT HAPPEN, AND A GREEN CASE PINS IT. scope.ts's new header concludes "nothing legitimately offerable disappears", and scope.test.ts case XP9 is named "a board rooted BELOW a plant still offers its own parts — nothing legitimately offerable disappears" and then asserts:

``ts expect(offered).not.toContain("Housing A"); ``

The reasoning above it is right about the fail-open→fail-closed CHANGE (it moves nothing) and then generalises to the picker as a whole, which it never examined: it says such a part "was already offered nowhere (XP2) — this change does not move it", which is true, and treats that as acceptable rather than as the pre-existing fault it is. The case is therefore green while asserting the opposite of what the server answers — CLAUDE.md §4's "a green case can be pinning the bug", in its literal form. XP9's third assertion has to change with the fix, and the file header's conclusion with it, or the next reader will believe the picker is proven correct for every role.

The developer's F-080 offers the rule "ask what narrowed the map". That rule is right for the board's map and is not enough here: for a supervisor the map is narrowed by a VIEW CHOICE and the place list was ALREADY narrowed by PERMISSION, one layer up, and by the time productOfferedAt sees [] the two are indistinguishable. No amount of care inside scope.ts can recover it.

No pin is filed, and the reason is worth stating: the obvious pin — asserting productOfferedAt([], path, map) === true — would be WRONG. It would break the honest zero (a part assigned to no plant is offered nowhere) that XP2 and history.ts's synthesised deleted product both depend on. The module cannot be fixed from inside; whatever the fix is, the pin belongs beside it.

The shape that suggests itself, and it is CLAUDE.md §4's own rule: stop shipping the raw place list to the client for this decision and let board_window answer the question the server would answer — a per-node offered-product id list, or a boolean computed through app_product_offered_at, which is already SECURITY DEFINER precisely so that "what a constraint may ASK does not depend on what the writer may LIST". The alternative — widening product_sites_select so a reader may see a place that is an ancestor of a node they can read — changes a read policy and would need its own cross-tenant case; the developer decides.

Developer note

Fixed in 808a52d by moving the question rather than sharpening the guess, which is the first of the two shapes this report offered. Migration 0042 adds app_offered_product_nodes — the set form of app_product_offered_at, SECURITY DEFINER — and board_window now carries offered_node_ids per product: the nodes in THIS window where a run would be accepted. productOfferedAt and productsOfferedHere are gone; the picker is membership in the server's answer, with no path logic left to disagree with it. site_node_ids keeps its old meaning and its old readers.

The other option in the report — widening product_sites_select — was not taken, and the reason is in the migration's header: it changes a read policy and would hand a line supervisor the existence of a plant she has no grant on, where answering the question changes no policy and discloses strictly less.

⭐ THE SECOND HALF OF THIS REPORT WAS THE MORE USEFUL ONE and is accepted without reservation. XP9 was green while asserting the opposite of what the server answers, and the proof it rested on was sound about the change it described and then generalised to the picker as a whole, which it never examined. Both are gone with the path logic they were about. The limit on F-080's rule is now written into scope.ts's header: "ask what narrowed the map" can only be applied by someone who knows what narrowed it, and here the map was narrowed by a view choice while the place list had already been narrowed by permission one layer up.

⚠️ NO VITEST PIN, DELIBERATELY, AND THE REPORT'S REASONING FOR THAT STILL HOLDS. The proof belongs where the authority is: supabase/tests/65_offered_in_window_ test.sql, whose X1 is this defect in one comparison — the same person, part and cell, with the readable place list EMPTY and the server answering TRUE — and whose X2 asserts that all four parts on her board now name her cell, while site_node_ids stays empty (so a later "fix" that widened the read policy instead would move that number and be caught). X4–X6 hold the DEFINER function to disclosing nothing the caller could not already read. Eight for eight against a database built from the harness, every migration and the seed.

Driven in the browser as Ana: four parts offered where one was. The company admin's board still lists exactly its own plant's four, so DEF-0002 has not come back.

Re-verified 2026-09-04 (tester)

Green at 808a52d. My reproduction was a screen and a server predicate; both were run cold, and I compared the two against each other rather than reading either alone.

The picker and the server now agree exactly, at three (role, cell) pairs. The comparison is the picker's own options against app_product_offered_at — the predicate the write guard runs — asked over that role's own session:

``` Ana (supervisor, Plant A / Line 1) — plant_a.area_1.line_1.cell_1 picker offers : ["Housing A","Bracket A","Line 1 Subassembly A","Common Fastener"] server accepts: ["Housing A","Bracket A","Line 1 Subassembly A","Common Fastener"] MATCH

Admin (company) — plant_a.area_1.line_1.cell_1 picker offers : ["Housing A","Bracket A","Line 1 Subassembly A","Common Fastener"] server accepts: ["Housing A","Bracket A","Line 1 Subassembly A","Common Fastener"] MATCH

Admin (company) — plant_a.area_2.line_3.cell_5 picker offers : ["Housing A","Bracket A","Area 2 Frame A","Common Fastener"] server accepts: ["Housing A","Bracket A","Area 2 Frame A","Common Fastener"] MATCH ```

One out of four became four out of four for Ana, and Cell 5 correctly swaps "Line 1 Subassembly A" for "Area 2 Frame A", so the within-plant precision the old path compare had was not lost when the path compare went.

DEF-0002 has not come back. In Ana's payload the read policy is visibly unchanged — site_node_ids is still empty for the three plant-wide parts, and the answer arrives beside it:

`` PN-1001 site_node_ids=0 offered_node_ids=3 PN-1002 site_node_ids=0 offered_node_ids=3 PN-1003 site_node_ids=1 offered_node_ids=3 PN-9001 site_node_ids=0 offered_node_ids=3 ``

and on the company admin's Plant A board every Plant B and Plant C part carries offered_node_ids=0. So the wider read policy really was not taken.

The new SECURITY DEFINER function does not disclose more than the caller could already read. p_root_path is a boundary the caller chooses, so I asked Ana's session for roots she holds no grant on:

`` app_offered_product_nodes(p_root_path), asked by Ana: plant_a.area_1.line_1 pairs=12 nodes=3 not readable by her=0 plant_a.area_1 pairs=12 nodes=3 not readable by her=0 plant_a pairs=12 nodes=3 not readable by her=0 plant_b pairs=0 nodes=0 not readable by her=0 plant_c pairs=0 nodes=0 not readable by her=0 plant_d pairs=0 nodes=0 not readable by her=0 ``

Widening the root gains her nothing: she gets her own three nodes and no more, and the other plants in her own org — the harder case, since org scoping alone would not catch it — come back empty.

Two mutations, both caught by name. Reverting productsOfferedAtNode to offer everything reds XP3, XP4, XP5 and XP6. Reverting it to read siteNodeIds — this defect, exactly — reds XP1, XP2, XP5, XP6 and XP7, and XP1 is named "the filed defect: a part whose places were filtered away is still offered". The behaviour is held in both directions, not incidentally green.

No pin existed, so there is nothing to promote. R-239 goes to covered; it now has the vitest and SQL verifiers it had never had.

The developer note's account of the second half of the report is accepted: XP9 and the header conclusion it rested on are gone with the path logic they were about.

DEF-0006 · major · doc-drift · verified

The demo world stops building: dev_demo.sql still writes runs.status, which migration 0044 dropped

Violates R-D112 · filed 2026-09-04 by tester · fix b4b31cc · pinned by src/test/defects/DEF-0006.test.ts

Reproduction

dev_demo.sql is not run by any script — it is applied by hand after a reset, and db:reset wipes the demo world every time (design-plan §19.72), so it is run again every time. Against any database that has migrations 0043 and 0044:

`` docker exec -i -e PGPASSWORD=postgres supabase_db_production_scheduler \ psql -U postgres -d <db> -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 -f - < supabase/dev_demo.sql ``

I ran it against the throwaway sql_test_db (harness + every migration + seed), never against the running dev database, and rebuilt the scratch database afterwards.

Or, with no database at all:

`` npx vitest run src/test/defects/DEF-0006.test.ts ``

Expected

R-D112: "dev_demo.sql clears org 1's seeded content and builds three identically shaped plants (Plant A/B/C → Area → Line → Cell) through create_node, with every row owned ... no run using a product owned outside it, and one supervisor (Ana) granted a single line."

It has to reach the runs to do that.

Actual

It gets as far as the runs and stops:

`` RESET DO CREATE TABLE DO INSERT 0 3 INSERT 0 3 DO DO DO DO DO psql:<stdin>:431: ERROR: column "status" of relation "runs" does not exist LINE 1: ...NTO runs (org_id, node_id, product_id, timerange, status, pl... ^ QUERY: INSERT INTO runs (org_id, node_id, product_id, timerange, status, planned_headcount) VALUES (v_org, v_cell, v_prod, tstzrange(v_day + (v_d || ' days')::interval + interval '6 hours', v_day + (v_d || ' days')::interval + interval '14 hours'), 'planned', 1) RETURNING id CONTEXT: PL/pgSQL function inline_code_block line 23 at SQL statement ``

⚠️ Note where it stops. The plants, structures, operators and products are already built; the runs and their assignments are not. So the failure leaves a HALF-BUILT demo world rather than refusing cleanly — the app comes up, the five demo sign-ins work, and the board is simply empty, which looks like a product with no data rather than a fixture that did not finish.

The pin is red on this build:

`` FAIL src/test/defects/DEF-0006.test.ts > does not write runs.status AssertionError: expected [ Array(1) ] to deeply equal [] + [ + "INSERT INTO runs (org_id, node_id, product_id, timerange, status, planned_headcount)", + ] ``

Its other two cases pass, which is the part worth reading: the guard case finds the inserts (so it is not passing by finding nothing), and assignments.status is already clean — migration 0043 dropped that column too and this file never wrote it, so the audit is telling the two apart rather than flagging the word.

Lead

A suspicion, not a fact.

supabase/dev_demo.sql:414 is the only line involved:

``sql INSERT INTO runs (org_id, node_id, product_id, timerange, status, planned_headcount) VALUES (..., 'planned', 1) ``

status has a default and the demo only ever wrote 'planned', so dropping the two columns from the statement should be the whole change. The rest of the file does not name either column — I checked, and the pin's third case asserts it.

⚠️ WHY NOTHING WENT RED. dev_demo.sql is on no runner's path. scripts/run-sql-test.sh and verify-db.sh build their database from the harness, the migrations and seed.sql; the vitest suite never touches SQL; and R-D112 has verified_by: [], so the one requirement that describes this file had nothing standing behind it. Migration 0044 was careful about the four functions and the exclusion constraint that named the column — it says so, and I checked each by extraction — and the fixture outside the migration set was the copy nobody grepped. That is CLAUDE.md §4's "after deleting a concept, grep the tests for its words", with a file that is not a test.

The narrow fix is one line. The wider question, which is the developer's: whether dev_demo.sql should be on a runner at all. It is the only fixture with three plants and a line-scoped supervisor, it is what the browser sees, and it is where every screen check this role runs starts from — including the ones that found DEF-0002 and DEF-0005, neither of which the seed's single plant could have shown.

Developer note

I agree with the diagnosis and the severity, and the fix is the one line the Lead names. Two corrections to the record, neither of which lets the defect off.

⚠️ dev_demo.sql IS run by a script, and that makes this worse rather than better. The Reproduction says it "is not run by any script — it is applied by hand after a reset". supabase/config.toml has sql_paths = ["./seed.sql", "./dev_demo.sql"], and its own header block records why that line was added: db:reset used to wipe the demo world and a manual paste into Studio was the only way back. So npm run db:reset has been applying this file, and failing inside it, every time — no hand step required, and no error a person had to be watching for. The premise the defect rests on is the narrower and correct one, which the Lead states exactly: it is on no test runner's path.

Where it stops is the part I want on the record. The file's §8 opens with "a demo file that half-ran is worse than one that failed" and then makes twelve assertions — including v_runs <> 36, which is precisely this bug. They never fired, because they sit at the FOOT and the file died at line 414. An assertion block cannot catch a fixture that does not reach it, so the fixture had to go on a runner where the APPLY itself is the test. That is what --demo does.

⚠️ AND THE LIVE DEV DATABASE IS NOT BROKEN, WHICH CHANGES THE URGENCY. Session 55's summary says "the live dev database is already showing 0 runs across all three plants, and dev_demo is the only thing that creates them." I measured it: org 1 has 36 runs and 39 assignments, and FOUR roots -- Plant A, B, C and a Plant D that is not in this fixture. The demo world was built while status still existed; 0044 dropped the column and kept the 36 'planned' rows, and dev_demo.sql has not been re-applied since. So nothing on this machine was ever half-built. The breakage was always in the FUTURE tense -- the next db:reset -- which is a weaker claim than the one filed and still a major defect, because that reset was going to happen and nothing would have said why.

⛔ A CONSEQUENCE WORTH KNOWING BEFORE ANYONE RESETS: Plant D and the three extra assignments are hand-made and live only in that database. dev_demo.sql opens by deleting org 1, so the next reset takes them whether or not this fix is in. That is the file working as designed, not a new bug, but it is the maintainer's call and not a session's.

Fix

supabase/dev_demo.sql no longer names runs.status.

supabase/tests/dev_demo_test.sql is new and is what R-D112 was missing — it builds the demo world on a database of its own and asks it eleven questions from outside the fixture, including the two nothing counted: the ASSIGNMENTS (the statement immediately after the one that broke) and ANA'S SINGLE LINE. scripts/run-sql-test.sh --demo builds sql_demo_db for it and drops it after; it cannot share sql_test_db, because dev_demo.sql deletes org 1's seeded content and about eighteen cases in eight files need that content intact. scripts/tester-run.mjs --sql now runs it.

Measured both ways rather than assumed. With the fix: 11 passed, 0 failed, 0 hard-errors. With the bug deliberately put back: the mode exits 1 and prints the column "status" of relation "runs" does not exist error verbatim. And the eleven cases were run against the seed-only world to check they are capable of failing at all — nine go red there, including D6 (runs=8 assignments=12) and D8 (Ana's seed grant is depth 2, an Area, not depth 3, a Line); the three that pass are invariants true of both worlds, not vacuous cases.

DEF-0007 · major · stale-refusal · verified

A plant admin cannot reach the Settings tab, and the server would take their writes on their own plant

Violates R-239 · filed 2026-09-05 by tester · fix 23ba9aa · pinned by src/test/auditAccess.test.tsx

Reproduction

In the running app (a dev server on the committed tip a4e938a):

`` POST /rest/v1/rpc/set_node_setting { "p_node_id": "<Plant A>", "p_key": "eligibility_policy", "p_value": "__probe__" } ``

An illegal value on purpose: set_node_setting checks PERMISSION before it looks at the value, so the refusal that comes back says which gate stopped her, and nothing is written either way.

Expected

R-239: "A client control is never hidden for something the server permits, and never shown for something the server refuses."

> ⚠️⚠️ A SITE ADMIN REACHES THIS PANE — adminSectionsFor returns "all" for > anyone with adminAnywhere === true, and a site admin holds an admin GRANT. > On the company scope they see both controls DISABLED with the reason in words > ... on THEIR OWN plant they get working pickers, because > set_node_setting's gate is app_is_admin() or app_is_admin_for(node). Three states, all implemented, none of them reachable.

invalid_argument, not not_permitted. The permission gate ran and passed her; only the deliberately illegal value stopped the write. A legal one would have been stored.

What turned up

93 things that weren't in the plan

The label named more than the button did · fixed · you found it · S22

"Edit" opened half of what it named, on two tabs

The maintainer, 2 Sept: on Shifts, "the edit button on the shift doesn't actually open the whole tab, it only opens to rename it, is very misleading"; on Products, "hitting edit does not open the option to change colours. Feels wrong and non-intuitive." Right on both.

Two earlier decisions had each scoped "Edit" deliberately narrow and moved the rest elsewhere: D106 made the pattern's Edit a name-and-owner control, with the shifts behind a separate click on the pattern name; D115 made the product's Edit code-and-name, with the colour on a clickable swatch. Each was defensible in isolation, and together they taught the reader that "Edit" meant "edit some of it" — and left the most-wanted thing (the shift times, the colour) down a path they had to already know about.

The fix follows the label, not the old scoping. A pattern's Edit now also expands the pattern, so name, owner, shifts and breaks all open at once; a product's Edit now shows the colour palette inside the edit panel, the swatch kept as a shortcut. One existing shift test had opened the pattern with a second click that now toggles it shut, so that step is gone — what it defends is unchanged. Both driven in the browser as the company admin.

D106 · D115

Found by driving it as the real roles · fixed · you found it · M5

Everyone but the system admin had no hierarchy filters on the matrix

The maintainer reported it in the middle of building M5: "site admins and supervisors have no hierarchy level filters in the matrix tab, only the system admin has them." Exactly right, and the cause was a quiet one.

The matrix's area/line filters were anchored on the plant chooser's value. But that chooser only appears for a reader who can see more than one plant — a company/system admin. Anyone with a single readable plant (every site admin, every supervisor) has the chooser hidden, and the shared plant-filter code correctly resolves their choice to null. Anchoring the cascade on that null meant they got no area or line filter at all — the very readers a supervisor view is for.

The fix is to stop asking "what plant did they pick" and ask "what is the top of what they can see". A new readableRootIds finds the nodes with no readable parent — which is a supervisor's own area just as much as an admin's whole plant — and cascadeBaseId hangs the cascade off the sole such root when there is no explicit choice. The multi-plant admin at "All plants" is deliberately left with no cascade until they pick a plant, because "Area 1" in three different plants is ambiguous. Proven both ways: pure tests on the two functions, and driving the screen as a site admin and a supervisor, who now each see a filter labelled with their hierarchy's own level word.

§19.86

The same mistake twice in one day, in one file · fixed · found while building · S22

A list of columns written out twice, and the second copy empties a screen in silence

This happened, was fixed, and then very nearly happened again four hours later in the same file. Asking the database for a training means naming the columns you want; checking what comes back means naming them again. When a column was added, the named list was updated and a second, older copy of the same list three hundred lines away was not. The check then rejected every row that came back, because it was missing a column nobody had asked for.

★★ The screen renders completely empty, and nothing anywhere reports an error. All 1,276 checks were passing while it was true. They could not see it: the screen tests hand the checker rows they made up themselves, rather than rows the database read actually requested. One side of the mismatch is a piece of text, the other is hand-written code, and nothing compares them.

It is last week’s lesson with the arrow reversed. Then a database change made a column optional and broke a hand-written check; this time a stricter check broke a hand-written list. **Either half can move, and the failure always looks like “there is no data” rather than like something breaking.

★ The second occurrence was caught by looking, not by testing. Having fixed it once I searched for the pattern, and there it was on the next table down — about to do the same thing to every training a person holds. A column list that appears twice is a bug with a delay on it.** The new check builds its test data from the list itself, so the two cannot drift apart again.

D114

You spotted a flicker · it was the cache · fixed · you found it

Switching tabs was quietly throwing away everything the app had loaded

You noticed the plant chooser blink for a fraction of a second when moving between tabs, and guessed it was the chooser. Right about where, one layer off about why — and the real answer was doing a lot more damage than a blink.

Switching tabs was clearing the entire data cache. The code that answers “who is signed in” kept its record of the current person separately in each part of the screen, each starting from “nobody”. Six parts ask that question now — so every freshly opened tab compared “nobody” against you, concluded somebody had just signed in, and threw everything away. The chooser needs the list of places to know how many plants you have; for one frame there was no list, so it removed itself.

★ It only showed for you. Anybody who can see one plant has no chooser at all, so there was nothing to blink — and their cache was being discarded silently and paid for in extra loading nobody could see.

★★ Measured rather than argued, before and after: the app was driven in a real browser and sampled every frame. Seven frames out of 235 had no chooser; afterwards, zero out of 236. The clearing itself is right and stays — signing in as somebody else must drop the last person’s data. What was wrong is who gets to say the person changed, and a part of the screen opening is not that.

A rule you set, that the database already had · fixed · found while building · S22

Supervisors needed no new permission — the one that decides scheduling already said it

You asked for supervisors to keep the training record, and then narrowed it yourself: “the supervisor is assigned to a line, so can give them control only for the line they’re assigned to?”

★★ That rule already existed, and it is the same one that decides who may put work on a cell. An assignment covers the place it names and everything under it, never anything above — so “assigned to a line” already meant that line and its cells. Nothing new had to be invented, which matters more than it sounds: a second, parallel idea of “may I act here” is two rules to keep in step, and they drift.

★ Two checks are what make it real, and either alone proves nothing. Somebody with the supervisor title but no assignment is refused; somebody with the assignment but only view rights is refused. Delete half the rule and one of those two still passes — which is exactly how a permission check quietly stops checking.

⚠ And it created a new problem, which is now also fixed. A line supervisor can see the plant’s trainings and cannot change them — so the screen was offering Rename and Retire on rows the database would refuse. The same “screen says yes, database says no” defect you found on the Operators screen, arriving from the other direction the moment supervisors were let in.

D114

Two columns in two weeks that existed and did nothing · fixed · found while building · S22

Half the sign-off was already in the database, with a note saying nothing reads it

Stage 22 needed two facts about a training somebody holds: when it was done and who signed it off. The first one had been sitting in the database for months, with a comment beside it reading, accurately, “nothing in this app reads it”. So the change added one field, not two.

★ That is the second time in two weeks. The on/off switch for trainings shipped the same way last week — a real column with no screen behind it, which is why “retire instead of deleting” was unavailable until the Trainings tab was built. Two is a pattern, not a coincidence: it is worth going looking for the others rather than finding them one at a time when a feature happens to need them.

Worth saying because it cuts the other way too: the honest reason both existed was that somebody added the column while building the thing next to it and left the screen for later. That is not wrong — it is how the delete warning could offer “deactivate instead” the moment a screen existed. It just needs writing down somewhere that gets read.

★★ So I went and counted, instead of leaving it as a worry. Every column in the database, checked against every line of the app: 94 columns, and exactly two that the app never mentions — both on the audit trail, which is written and never read back by design. There is no third one waiting. The pattern was two, both are now on screen, and it is closed rather than open. That took one command and replaces a standing suspicion with a number.

D114

My own sequencing · you had to ask twice · fixed · you found it · S22

I built the plumbing under a screen and never built the screen

Your words: “I thought we were going to create a trainings tab like operator/shifts/products. These should be editable and we’re still calling them tickets.” You were right, and nothing you said had been unclear.

The plan had the tab queued as stage 22, behind stage 21. I worked the list in the order it was written and never checked the order was still yours. Two things made that worse, and both are mine. I had been saying “trainings” back to you for a dozen messages while the screen still said “Tickets” — which on its own reads as if the rename were done. And when you said “trainings can be added by individual site admins, but only to their own sites”, I heard a question about permissions and answered with a database rule. It reads just as naturally as a statement about a screen you expected to exist — because “a site admin adds a training” meant, that day: open Operators, pick some person, expand a toggle.

The database work was not wasted — two plants genuinely could not both have a “Forklift”, and the starter set was dead without fixing it. But you asked for the screen and I delivered the pipework under it. The standing correction: a queue’s order is a guess about priority, not a fact, and it is cheap to ask.

D104

Found by driving the real app · fixed · you found it

The plant chooser blinked on every tab change — and the chooser was not the problem

You spotted this and guessed it was the filter’s placeholder. Right about where, one layer off about why. I drove the running app in a real browser and sampled every frame: the chooser’s row vanished and came back within one or two frames on every single tab switch, and everything below it jumped up and dropped back down.

What was actually happening: switching tabs was throwing away the whole data cache. The code that answers “who is signed in” kept its record of the current person per component, starting from “nobody”. Six components ask that question now — so every freshly opened panel compared “nobody” against you, concluded somebody had just signed in, and cleared everything. The chooser needs the site list to know how many plants you have; for a moment there was no list, so the row removed itself.

★ It only showed for you, and that is why it looked like a chooser bug. Anybody who can see one plant has no chooser at all — so for them there was nothing to blink, and the cache was being thrown away silently and paid for in extra loading.

★★ Measured before and after rather than argued: seven frames out of 235 with no row, against zero out of 236. The clearing itself is right and stays — a real sign-in must drop the last person’s data. What was wrong is who gets to say an identity changed, and a component opening is not that.

I shipped it, an agent caught it · fixed · found while building · S22

A stricter safety net and a hand-written list of columns, disagreeing in silence

Adding the on/off flag to trainings meant asking the database for one more column and teaching the safety net to insist on it. There were two copies of that column list — a named one and an older hand-written one three hundred lines up — and I only updated the named one. The other kept asking for the old columns, so the net rejected every training that came back. **Both screens would have rendered completely empty.

★★ All 1,276 checks were green while that was true**, because the screen tests hand the safety net rows they invented rather than rows the database read actually asked for. Nothing else could see it: one side is a string, the other is hand-written code.

It is last week’s lesson with the arrow reversed. Then, a database change made a column optional and broke a hand-written check. This time a stricter check broke a hand-written column list. Either half can move, and the failure is always silent — it looks like “there is no data”, never like an error. The new check builds its test data from the column list itself, so the two cannot drift apart again.

D114

The demo data had been telling us for weeks · fixed · found while building · S21

Every training in the demo was named after its plant, and the comment said why

Training names had to be unique across the whole company, while a training belongs to one plant and you can only see your own. So the second plant to want a “Forklift” was refused — pointed at a row it had no way to open, edit or reuse.

★★ The sample data had been working around this since the day it was written, calling everything A-Welding, B-Welding, C-Welding, with a comment saying “names are unique per org, so they carry the plant letter”. **A fixture prefixing its way around a product rule is the loudest signal there is, and it sat in the repository unread for weeks.

★ You proposed the same workaround independently — put the plant in the name — which is what told me it was the natural reading. We did the other half instead: the plant is already stored in its own column, so it is shown rather than typed in, and it follows a rename for free. Dropping those prefixes from the demo is the proof it works**, and three lookups in that file had to learn the owner, because a name on its own had stopped being enough to identify anything.

§19.80 · D111a

My own process · I reported this one to you twice before checking it · fixed · found while building · S25

I filed a bug that wasn’t one, because I believed a comment instead of reading the code it described

I told you twice that the “belongs to” dropdowns on three screens were offering places the database would refuse to save to, and that fixing it was the obvious next job. It was not a bug. I had read the note attached to the shared helper — which says, persuasively, that a dropdown offering something the server will reject is a control whose only outcome is an error message — and filed it without opening the three screens that use it.

One of them had already tried it, measured the result, and undone it, with the reason written down at the spot: the only list the app can build of “places you administer” is derived from which structures you own, which is not the question the save actually asks — so a plant admin whose plant had no structure of its own was offered nothing at all. Its verdict: offering something the server then refuses costs one clear sentence; offering nothing costs the whole feature.

And there is no right answer available to build. The server can tell the app which structures you may edit, and whether you administer anything — but nothing that lists the places. So there was never a correct list to narrow to.

★★ What was actually wrong was the note, and the unused switch it was attached to. Nobody had ever used that switch; it sat there with a convincing explanation of why someone should. A dead option with a persuasive comment is not harmless — it argues for itself, and it argued me into two false reports. Both are gone, the reasoning is kept where they were, and the check that tested the switch now pins the rule that replaced it.

Worth saying plainly: this is the same mistake as the bold warning above, made in the other direction — last time I believed my own plan over the product, this time a comment over the code beside it.

§19.79

Stage 21 · you found it the moment it shipped · fixed · you found it · S21

The Hierarchy tab ended up with two dropdowns listing the same plants

Your words: “the Hierarchy tab still has 2 filters, what are we doing about it?” And it was worse than a duplicate, because the two lists literally read the same. When a new plant is created the app copies its structure — so that renaming a rung in Plant A does not rename it in B and C — and the copy is named after the plant. So the Site Structure chooser was offering “Plant A / Plant B / Plant C” directly underneath a header reading “Showing: Plant A”. In practice a structure is a plant, and that chooser was a plant chooser wearing another name.

It was my inconsistency, not a new question. The new filter narrows every other list and every other dropdown on every other tab; I simply missed this one. It now narrows too, and the selection is resolved against what the chooser actually offers — a choice must not outlive the list it was made from.

★ One structure is deliberately kept at every plant: the unowned original, which is the seed corn every new plant is copied from. Hiding it under a filter would leave you unable to build a new plant while looking at an old one.

★ And I checked which one it lands on rather than assuming. Narrowed to Plant A the list is “Plant A” and “Standard Plant”, sorted by name, so it opens on your plant’s own structure. Had it sorted the other way you would have been quietly editing the rungs of an empty structure.

§19.79

Three screens · one shape · fixed · found while building · S25

A dropdown showing one thing and saving another — three times, in three different screens

This is a browser behaviour worth knowing on sight, because nothing on screen looks wrong when it happens. Give a dropdown a stored value that is not one of the options it is currently offering, and it displays the first option and reports nothing at all. The box says one thing; the save sends another.

The plant filter was only the reason to look. All three were already there:

Products. Re-home a part to Plant 2, then filter to Plant 1: the owner box reads “Plant 1” and Save sends Plant 2.

Shift patterns. The “which pattern runs here” draft is stored per place but holds a pattern; with the pattern list narrowed, a row reads “Inherit from above” and Apply sends the pattern you can no longer see.

Operators. On a cold load the “belongs to” box showed the first plant while the form held nothing, so Add submitted an empty owner. ★ And the note beside it claimed a guard that did not exist — a comment describing a check nobody had written. It exists now.

The general rule: whenever a list a dropdown draws from can shrink, the held value has to be re-checked against it on every render. Three screens already had that check; three did not.

§19.79

My instruments · the test was the thing that was broken · fixed · a test found it · S25

A screen test that had been passing through the wrong half of the code for its entire life

The Products screen is the only one with a test that actually draws it, and it has been green from the day it was written. It was green for the wrong reason. The stand-in it uses for the database read was missing one flag — the one that says “the structure list arrived”. Without it, every single case ran the “the structure list failed to load” branch: no plant names, every row labelled “Another site”, and the count line it was supposed to be checking unreachable.

So eight checks named after behaviour they never once exercised. They did not fail; they quietly tested a different screen.

★★ The lesson is the sharpest one this project has about fakes: a stand-in that omits a field does not error, it silently picks a branch — and the suite then reports coverage it does not have. It is the same family as the run that once reported every deliberate breakage as uncaught: the number looked like a result and was a broken instrument. Fixed, the fixture rebuilt so cases stop editing each other’s data, and nine new checks added on top.

§19.79

My own reasoning · and you overturned it in one sentence · fixed · you found it · S20

I wrote a warning in bold, in the plan, arguing for the wrong answer

When the Operators screen was fixed I wrote, in bold: “the fix is NOT to hide the other plants” — reasoning that since a supervisor may place someone outside their area with a reason, hiding those places would delete the feature. **You read the result and said the opposite: “those locations should not be visible at all is my point.”

You were right, and the reason is worth keeping.** I had reasoned by analogy with the scheduling board, where the picker is anchored on a specific cell and must list every candidate — because listing them is how you place one. The admin screen places nothing. The same rule can be correct on one screen and wrong on the next, and an argument that travels between them without being re-checked is how a plan starts contradicting the product.

★★ The standing lesson: a bold warning in my own plan is a previous sitting’s argument, not evidence. I had inherited that line from the earlier note and treated it as settled. It is now recorded as a wrong argument rather than quietly deleted, because the reasoning error is the useful part.

§19.77

Stage 20 · your second look, same screen · fixed · you found it · S20

Labelling the other plants was not enough — they had to go

The first fix gave the list three honest answers instead of two: can work here, missing the training, and outside their area, allowed only with a recorded reason. You looked again and said the label was not the point — the rows should not be there at all.

The list now stops at the plant the person belongs to, for every person, not just the one you were looking at. What is trimmed is counted underneath — “12 places outside Plant A are not shown” — because a list that quietly shrank looks exactly like a person with no options.

★ The cut is the plant, not their own area, and that is what keeps the third answer alive. Lending somebody from Line 1 to Line 2 in the same plant is a thing supervisors do and is exactly what the override was built for. Cutting at their own line instead would have removed that possibility from the screen entirely.

★ Operator A1 is the only person in the demo who could have shown you this — every plant has six people, five belonging to the whole plant and one belonging to a single line. For the other five, both possible answers look identical. You happened to open the one that distinguishes them.

§19.77

Stage 20 · you found it in a minute · fixed · you found it · S20

The Operators screen told you someone can work in plants they are not allowed to enter

You opened a person who belongs to one line in Plant A, and the screen listed every cell in all three plants and ticked twelve of them. It asks each cell one question — does this person hold the trainings this cell needs? — and never asks the second one, which is whether they are allowed to be in that part of the plant at all. The only cells the database will actually accept are the two under their own line, and both of those are crosses. The true answer is zero places. The screen said twelve of eighteen.

The person’s own “belongs to” is sitting right there in the data the screen is handed. It is written into the type with a note explaining that it binds scheduling, and the loop underneath never reads it.

★★ This is the dangerous direction, and it is the opposite of the two stale rules I deleted yesterday. Those refused things the database allows — quietly annoying. This one permits things the database refuses. A supervisor plans a week from this screen and gets turned down at the moment they try to book it, with an error that says nothing about why the screen said yes. **A screen that looks like it works is the worst of the set.

★ And the fix is not to hide the other plants** — because of the decision you made yesterday, a supervisor may put someone outside their area with a reason. So the honest screen has three answers, not two: can work here, missing the training, and outside their area — allowed, but someone has to say why. The count has to name what it counts, too: “0 of 2 places in their own area”, with everything else listed below it. Written down as the next item; no database change needed.

§19.77

Stage 21 · the largest one yet · fixed · found while building · S21

One deleted part with history, and the board stops loading — for everyone

This is the feature from two days ago breaking the screen, and the feature is the one that was supposed to protect it. Keeping the past means that when a part is deleted, a run that already made it stops pointing at the part and keeps its name, code and colour instead. That is the whole point. But the app’s reader still demanded a part on every run — and when it meets a run without one it rejects that run, and rejecting one row makes it throw away the entire list. **Not a missing name on one bar. A blank board, for everybody in the company, with an error message about a data shape.

Nothing I own could have caught it. The compiler was clean, because the type definitions it reads are generated from the database and describe columns, not the hand-written check underneath. The database suite was green, because it never parses anything. The demo world has never had a delete run against it, so no screen ever met the shape. I found it because I opened that file to add an unrelated field.

The rule that comes out of it, and it is now written into the process:** a database change that makes a column optional is a change to the app, and it is the one kind the type generator will not surface — the app keeps compiling and starts refusing real rows. After any such change, search the app for that column by name and read every check that mentions it.

Underneath it sat the softer half of the same gap: even once the row loaded, every part of the board looked a part up by its link, and a deleted part has none — so it would have drawn as “(unknown product)” in grey, which is exactly the outcome remembering the name was added to prevent. The columns shipped and nothing read them. Both halves are fixed together, with twenty new checks over the piece that does the drawing.

§19.76

Stage 20 · the instrument, not the code · fixed · found while building · S20

A test can pass for a reason that has nothing to do with what it is testing

Fifteen deliberate breakages, and two of them came back “caught” when they had not been caught at all. The runner builds each broken copy by find-and-replace, and one of its own replacements was too eager — it rewrote a file path into one that does not exist. A run against a missing file produces no passing checks. No passing checks looks identical to every check failed, which is what “caught” means. So the instrument reported success for two breakages it never actually tested.

The reason this is worth writing down: I have a rule for the opposite case and it did not help here. When a breakage comes back uncaught I already treat it as a hole until proven otherwise. What I did not have was any suspicion of a caught verdict — the good news — and that is the one that quietly certifies work nobody tested. A pass can be as false as a failure, and it is much easier to accept. Both runs were redone against real files.

§19.76

Stage 20 · a test proving nothing · fixed · found while building · S20

Seventeen checks all answered correctly, and not one of them reached the rule

The new checks needed a person who can schedule on a cell but is not a company admin — the exact person the override is for. I gave them the wrong company-wide role, and the permission gate reads the company-wide role first, so every one of the seventeen was refused before it ever got near the area rule, and every one of them was refused with the answer the test expected. Green, and testing nothing. Two more had the same shape for a different reason: they read “no error” as “it worked” on an edit — but an edit the database refuses silently changes nothing and raises nothing. Those now read the row back and check it actually changed.

§19.76

Stage 20 · caught by auditing my own change · fixed · a test found it · S20

I retyped a routine instead of extracting it, and silently dropped three of its safety checks

Two of the three write paths needed two new arguments threaded through. For one of them I typed the routine out again from what I remembered of it — and the version I typed was missing its shape validation, its per-place permission checks, and one line that sets a starting value. It surfaced as a baffling error about a completely unrelated field. A second routine had the same treatment and I had not noticed at all: two permission checks that used to be one, now answering with two different sentences.

Fixed by asking the database to print the routine it is actually running and changing exactly the words that needed changing — with each change asserted to have matched something, so a change that silently matches nothing cannot pass as done. The third path had been extracted properly and was clean, which is the comparison that makes the point.

§19.76

Stage 21 · found by your run · fixed · you found it · S21

The number I hand you to check my work against had been wrong by two since last session

The whole convention is that I predict the test count and you check it matches exactly, because a different number means a file didn’t load. I predicted 1116; you got 1118. The gap was not a file failing to load — it was the number I was counting up from. Last session’s confirmed total was written down as 1105 and should have been 1107; nothing changed between, it was simply transcribed wrong.

Two is a small error and it is the worst possible kind here, because it doesn’t look like an error — it looks like a broken suite, every time, forever, and the real signal gets lost in it. Corrected in the plan and in my notes, and the rule I broke is now written down: when a run confirms a count, copy the runner’s own total line, never a number I reasoned my way to.

§19.74

Stage 21 · an audit catching me · fixed · a test found it · S21

I added a stylesheet to a folder that is audited and didn’t add it to the audit

The two red checks on your run were both this. There is a check that walks the admin folder and fails if any stylesheet in it is not on the list of files being audited for sizing — it exists because exactly this happened once before and went unnoticed until someone opened the app on a large monitor. It worked.

Worth saying plainly: this is the class of thing I cannot catch from here, because that suite only runs on your machine. What I could have done was search for the list when I created the file — the list names its own folder in a comment. That is now the standing move. The fix is measured rather than predicted: the list is complete, removing the new entry makes the audit name it again, and the new file has no unscaled sizes.

§19.74

Stage 21 · found by breaking it · fixed · a test found it · S21

I removed the permission check from the delete and every single test still passed

The routine that deletes a part checks up front whether you administer the site it belongs to. I took that check out to prove the tests would notice. They did not. Not because the check is useless — a second gate underneath still refuses the final step and it produces the same error code — but because the code was all anything was looking at.

What actually changes without it is the sentence you read: “you don’t have admin rights over this site” becomes “the product itself could not be deleted”, which tells you nothing you can act on, and it arrives after the work has been done and rolled back rather than before it was attempted. A check now reads the message, not just the code.

This is the point of breaking my own work on purpose. Fifteen of sixteen breakages were caught immediately; this was the one that wasn’t, and “not caught” with no explanation is a hole — so it got one.

§19.74

Stage 21 · a sentence, not a bug · fixed · found while building · S21

The app was still saying “company-wide” a day after company-wide stopped existing

A refusal message offered “Company-wide products can only be changed by a company admin.” That state was removed the day before. The compiler cannot see inside a sentence, so it said nothing — and there was a passing test that checked for the exact wrong words, which is worse than no test, because it reads as coverage.

Three more of the same kind turned up in the same pass, all found by searching for the words rather than by any tool: two places on the People screen that still drew a “company-wide” badge, and a note on the Shift Patterns screen telling you to detach a pattern before deleting it — which this change made untrue.

§19.74

Stage 21 · a rule the database no longer has · fixed · found while building · S21

The People screen refused to delete anyone holding a training, and told you to do work that no longer exists

It said “3 tickets are still attached to this person. Remove them first, or deactivate them instead.” That was correct when the database would have refused the delete anyway. It does not any more — a person’s trainings now go with them — so the screen was enforcing a rule the database does not have, and the way out it named was work nobody needed to do.

This is the more dangerous half of the pair above. A stale permission check fails loudly the first time somebody tries it. A stale refusal never fails at all: it quietly stops people doing something they are allowed to do, and nobody reading the screen has any way to find that out.

§19.74

Stage 21 · would have shipped broken · fixed · found while building · S21

Deleting a person would have failed because of a shift they had already worked

Keeping someone’s finished work means rewriting those rows to carry their name instead of a link to them. Rewriting a shift wakes up the check that stops anyone being booked past their capacity — and with nobody attached to the row any more, that check measured the shift against nothing and refused it. Anyone who had ever worked a full shift would have been undeletable, and the failure would have read as “delete is broken”, not as anything to do with capacity.

A second one of the same kind sat next to it: a safety check that reads “is this person allowed here” gave up early once nobody was attached, and skipped the “is this part allowed here” question underneath it. Neither is visible from the happy path; both have their own check now.

§19.74

Stage 19 · you told me three times · fixed · you found it · S19

I kept building the “add” form and leaving the “edit” form short

You could choose where a product belonged when you created it, and never change it afterwards. Same for a person. Same for a shift pattern. Three separate screens, the same mistake, and I did not see the pattern until you pointed at it for the third time — because from where I was looking the feature worked: the picker was there, on the form I had just built.

It is the same defect as the break you could only delete and retype, two stages back, wearing different clothes. And it is worse than a missing button, because it quietly says where this belongs is decided when it is born — which for a line that gets reorganised is simply untrue.

All three now have it on the edit form, and there is a check that fails the build if anything you can set when creating a row cannot also be changed afterwards. Not a note to myself — the same move as the drag colours in stage 13 and the panel switches in stage 16: make the rule structural rather than remembered.

§19.66 · §19.67 · D105

Stage 19 · my own new check reported a fault that did not exist · fixed · found while building · S19

The check I wrote to catch that mistake could not catch it

Two things wrong with it, both found by deliberately breaking working code to see whether it noticed. First it read the wrong slice of the file and reported a fault in code that was correct. Then, once that was fixed, I deleted the exact line it was written to protect and it stayed green — because a leftover type declaration still mentioned the field, and the check could not tell “this code writes the value” from “this code mentions the name”.

Worth saying because it is the whole argument for breaking things on purpose: a check that has only ever run against working code tells you nothing about whether it can fail. Half of its cases now run against deliberately broken versions.

§19.67

Stage 19 · it had been blocking you and neither of us saw it · fixed · you found it · S19

The Products screen asked the wrong question about who may edit a row

It decided by asking which structures you administer. The database decides by asking which places you administer. Those sound like the same question and are not: a site whose structure is shared with another site is deliberately left unclaimed by an older migration, so its admin dropped out of the first list entirely — every button greyed out, every one of their own products labelled as another site’s, while the server would have accepted every change they tried to make.

The failure was one-directional and it was the worst direction: a wrong “no” is invisible and permanent, because a screen that refuses looks exactly like a screen with nothing to do. It now lets you try and shows the server’s answer — which, since the error-message work two stages back, is a plain sentence rather than a shrug. The reason the default could flip is that we fixed the error messages first; before that, guessing wrong meant a screen that lied either way.

§19.64 · §19.67

Stage 19 · found by breaking the new check on purpose · fixed · found while building · S19

The colour check would have accepted “teal #1baf7a” as a colour

Letting you type a hex code means the database has to know what one looks like. I wrote that check, wrote five tests for spellings it must reject, and they all passed — then broke the check deliberately to see whether the tests would notice, and they did not. Every rejected spelling I had thought of was also the wrong LENGTH, so not one of them could tell “this string IS a colour” from “this string CONTAINS one”.

A string that holds a perfectly good hex and is not one is the only input that separates them — and it is exactly what a paste out of a design tool looks like.

§19.66
