Stage 19 · three copies of one rule, found while changing it · fixed · found while building · S19

“What colour is this product” was answered in three different files

The admin screen had the answer, the board grid had its own copy, and the board legend had a third — with a comment on the board copies saying they were kept in step with the admin one. That sentence is the definition of a rule held together by somebody remembering.

Adding the hex option is precisely the edit that breaks it: a hand-set colour would have been right on the settings screen and drawn as the default blue on the board, with all three files individually correct. This is the same defect as the drag colours in stage 13, and the same fix — move the rule somewhere everything can reach, rather than matching copies by hand. The reason there were three was a rule that the board may not borrow code from the settings screens; the answer was to put it where neither owns it.

§19.66

Stage 18 · you found it — and the code for it already existed · fixed · you found it · S18

A break could only be deleted and retyped, while the write to edit one sat unused

Moving a break by five minutes meant removing it and adding it again — a destructive action offered for a change that destroys nothing, and one that silently lost the break’s name along the way. The function to do it properly had been written, tested and given its own error handling, and nothing on screen ever called it.

That shape has now caught us twice. The first time it was a piece of state written once and never read; this time it is a piece of code exported and never imported. Both mean the same thing: a requirement was dropped somewhere between the plan and the screen, and the leftover is the fingerprint. Searching for it is close to free.

§19.65

Stage 18 · you found it · fixed · you found it · S18

Nothing said a shift pattern row could be opened

The pattern name was a button drawn with no border and no background — identical, pixel for pixel, to the plain text in the column next to it. Behind it sat every shift in that pattern. You found it by clicking on the off-chance; someone new would have concluded the screen was empty.

An earlier round of this project established that a row must say what it is — that is why every level row now names its level instead of relying on indentation. This is the same rule arriving from the other side: a row must also say what it does. Neither is visible to any test.

§19.65

Stage 18 · a table that was pretending to be one · fixed · found while building · S18

My first fix for the breaks list had the same bug as the thing it was fixing

You asked why breaks weren’t tabular. I made them a table — and set the name column to take a share of whatever width was going, which stretched it across the entire panel and left “Lunch” six hundred pixels from “10:00–10:30”. Columns that far apart are not a table; you still have to read each line on its own. It is the identical mistake that had thrown a break’s “Remove” link to the far right edge the day before, which I had just finished fixing.

A short list should occupy the width it needs and stop. The only thing that caught it was rendering the page and looking at it.

§19.65

Stage 18 · a sentence that outlived what it described · fixed · found while building · S18

The hint under the product form said colours never change — one line above the new colour picker

Making the swatch clickable made that sentence false. It is a small thing with a sharp edge: a hint describing the old behaviour is worse than no hint at all, because it actively tells someone the control in front of them does not exist, and they will believe the words over the button. Changed in the same edit.

§19.65

Stage 18 · found by breaking my own new tests · fixed · found while building · S18

Two different complaints about the same shift were being treated as the same complaint

When you edit a shift, the screen shows only the problems your edit caused. To do that it has to recognise which problems were already there — and it identified them by which shift and which break they belonged to, but not by what kind of problem they were. So a shift that already had a naming complaint could be given an illegal time, and the new complaint would be mistaken for the old one and swallowed. The save would have gone through silently.

Nothing found this by reading. I broke the identification rule on purpose to check the new tests would notice, they didn’t, and that turned out to be a real hole rather than a harmless change.

§19.65

Stage 17 · my call, not yours — and I explained it badly · owed · found while building · S17

Long lists get cut off at 1,000 rows and the app is not told

Plainly: if you ever have more than a thousand of something, the app quietly gets the first thousand and believes that is all of them. More than a thousand people. More than a thousand products. More than a thousand training records — which is only about 350 people with three trainings each, so that is the one you would reach first.

Why it is worse than a truncated list. The Operators screen does not just display data, it works something out from it — it takes your people, their trainings, and what each cell requires, and decides who can work where. If a training record gets cut off, Ben shows a red cross and “missing CNC” for a cell he is fully trained for, and nobody schedules him there again. If a requirement gets cut off instead, the opposite: a green tick for a cell the system will refuse the moment you try to book it. The screen looks completely normal either way.

You do not need to decide anything, and asking you to was my mistake. I offered you two options that were both just “how” — neither was a business question. Here is what I will do: fetch long lists in batches until there are none left, so nothing is ever silently cut; and where the app is working something out rather than just listing, have it say so plainly instead of showing a confident answer built on incomplete data.

What you would notice today: nothing, because nothing here is near a thousand rows. It matters the day a real plant is loaded, and it is far cheaper to fix before that than after.

§19.64

Stage 17 · an agent I asked to break it found this · fixed · a reviewer found it · S17

A shift ending at midnight made one of its own breaks impossible to enter

A shift that runs 16:00 to midnight is stored as “16:00 to minute 1440”, and the screen correctly labelled it 16:00–00:00 +1d. But a second piece of the same code, asked “does this shift cross midnight?”, said no — off by exactly one minute. That answer is what decides whether the break form offers you the “+1 day” tick-box. Without the tick-box there is no way to type midnight at all: a clock field will not accept “24:00”. So the perfectly ordinary 23:45–00:00 wash-up break was unenterable — and our own validation refused it with “this break ends before it starts”, on a row the database would have accepted without complaint.

The thing to notice is that one object was answering the same question three different ways and no test compared them to each other. One check that says “these three must agree” would have caught it. That check now exists.

§19.64

Stage 17 · a label that quietly meant two different things · fixed · found while building · S17

Two different breaks, printed identically — and every after-midnight break read as twenty hours early

The rule was “put the +1d marker on the end of a span only, never the start”, and it was justified by a real database rule: a shift cannot start on the following day. True — of shifts. The same piece of code also prints breaks, and breaks have no such rule; the sample data itself has three of them starting after midnight. So a break running 02:00–02:30 the next morning printed as “02:00–02:30 +1d”, which by the screen’s own convention says it starts at two in the morning on the shift’s own day — twenty hours before the shift begins. Worse, two genuinely different, both-storable breaks printed the identical string.

The comment explaining the shortcut wasn’t wrong. It was true about the table it was written for and quietly assumed about the second one.

§19.64

Stage 17 · a door that locked behind you · fixed · you found it · S17

One stray break made a shift’s times permanently uneditable — and blamed the break

When you edit something, the screen tries to show you only the problems your edit caused, not everything already wrong with the pattern. Sensible. It worked out which problems were “already there” by comparing the sentences — and the sentence for a break that sits outside its shift contains the shift’s own times. So changing the shift’s times rewrote that sentence, the screen decided your edit had created a new problem, and refused to save. Every time. The only way out was to delete a break you may not have wanted to delete, because the screen offers no way to move one.

And the error it showed you named the break, not the times you had just changed. Problems are now identified by which shift and which break they belong to; the sentence is what we show, not what we compare.

§19.64

Stage 17 · the safety net had a hole where it mattered · fixed · found while building · S17

“You don’t administer a site” — told to a site admin, because a different read had failed

The Products screen is deliberately cautious: if it cannot confirm which sites you run, it goes read-only rather than offering buttons that will fail. That is the right choice, and there was a message written for exactly that case — “we couldn’t check which sites you administer, so this list is read-only for now.”

The message could never appear. It was gated on the check having come back, and the situation it existed for is precisely the one where the check does not come back. So in that case the screen fell through to a different sentence: “You don’t administer a site, so there’s nowhere to add a product” — a flat untruth, told to someone whose changes the server would have accepted, with every one of their own products simultaneously labelled as belonging to another site, and nothing on screen offering to try again.

A cautious fallback whose explanation cannot render is indistinguishable from a lie. It now says the true thing, and it also waits for that check before drawing anything — the same dead screen used to flash briefly on every normal load.

§19.64

Stage 17 · found by looking at the picture · fixed · found while building · S17

A break’s “Remove” sat a few pixels from the shift’s “Delete”

Two destructive actions, on the same line of sight, at the far right edge — and the one that removed a break was nowhere near the break it belonged to, because it inherited a spacing rule written for the row above it. Nothing was broken; it just invited the wrong click. It now sits beside its own break.

In the same picture: the sentence that warns you a delete is about to be refused was drawn in the app’s muted grey, which measures 3.5 to 1 against its background — below the readability floor for text that size. Fine for a column heading, not fine for the one line telling you why the button you are about to press will not work.

§19.64

Stage 16 · all three surveys found the same thing · fixed · found while building · S16

“You need to sign in to do that” — said to someone who is signed in

Before building the three screens I sent three agents to survey them, one each, reading the database as the authority. They came back agreeing on something none of them was sent to find: none of these three screens has a single database function behind it. They write their tables directly, and the permission rules from stage 14 are the only gate. That is fine and it is how the rest of the app already works — but the part of the app that turns a database refusal into a sentence was written when every write went through a function, so it had no words for any of this. I measured all five on a real database:

A site admin creating something company-wide was told “You need to sign in to do that.” — which is how someone ends up signing out and back in to fix a permission they simply do not have. A site admin editing a row that isn’t theirs got “Saved.” and nothing changed — because a permission rule that hides a row doesn’t raise an error, it just quietly matches nothing. A duplicate product code and a delete blocked by existing work both said “Something went wrong.” And two overlapping shifts would have said “Someone else changed this run first”, because that particular error had exactly one possible cause when the code was written, and a shifts screen makes that false.

All three lanes would have papered over the same five holes in three different ways. Fixed once instead, before any of them starts — the same argument as the groundwork commit, arriving from a different direction. 18 new checks, 11 deliberate breakages, all 11 caught, and every test uses a real error message captured from the database rather than one I typed.

§19.63

Stage 16 · found while clearing the way for the next three screens · fixed · found while building · S16

A menu item that opens onto nothing, four times over

The admin screen keeps two lists that have to agree: the one that draws the buttons down the left side, and the one that decides what each button shows. Nothing connected them. Adding a button without its screen would have produced a menu item that opens onto a blank pane — and the commit I was writing adds four buttons at once, so it was four of those waiting to happen.

There is now a check that reads both lists out of the file and fails if any button has nothing behind it. One of its six deliberate breakages is worth a line: a sloppier way of finding the list produces exactly the same answer today and would start being wrong the moment someone adds an unrelated list underneath it. That one needed a test written specially, or the boundary would have quietly re-opened later. Nothing visible changed in this commit — that is the point of it.

§19.62

Stage 15 · I had already found this, and written it down · fixed · found while building · S15

A note that never became code — for a whole day, and nothing noticed

Before writing stage 03's database side I spent an afternoon measuring what it does when it goes wrong, and one of the things I measured was this: moving something into a place that already has something of that name comes back as a raw database error, with no explanation attached. I wrote the finding down in the design document, in a table, with the fix beside it. Then I shipped stage 03 without the fix.

Thirty-three tests passed. They kept passing for a day. The structural reason is the part worth keeping: every one of those tests asked what a legal move does, and not one asked what a refusal looks like. A test suite written that way is complete about behaviour and completely silent about what you would have seen on screen. Every refusal this project shows a user now gets a test that checks the message it arrives with, not just that it arrived.

§19.61

Stage 15 · found by building the screen · fixed · found while building · S15

The most important thing this feature says would have read “Something went wrong”

The whole point of moving something to a different rung being refusable is the case where work is already booked on it: “you can’t move this, there are three shifts on it — move them first.” The database was saying exactly that. It was packing it in a shape the app doesn’t know how to open, so what would have reached you is “Something went wrong. Please try again.”

Every other place in the system that refuses for this reason packs it the same way as each other; this one function packed it differently, and no test compared them. Both are fixed in one small database change, and neither invents a new kind of error — they are the existing rules being kept where they were quietly being broken.

§19.61

Stage 15 · two of my own new tests were wrong · fixed · found while building · S15

A test whose name claimed something its own setup couldn’t produce

Two of the 36 new checks failed the first time I ran them, and neither was a bug in the code. One said “a target is never the thing itself or anything under it” against a node that couldn’t get that far — something under it had nowhere to go, so it was refused before any list was built. The other said “nothing at the same rung to move it under” while actually testing “no rung below”.

A test name is a claim, and the setup has to be able to deliver it. Both are rewritten with a note saying what they were before, so the next person doesn’t reintroduce the comfortable version.

§19.61

Stage 14 · an agent I asked to break it found this · fixed · a reviewer found it · S14

The colour picker could be asked about another company’s products

The new change gives each product a colour, and picks a sensible default by looking at what colours are already in use. That look-up ran with elevated privileges — it has to, to see rows the person can’t — and it took the company to look in as an argument. Anyone signed in could have handed it a different company’s identifier and learned something about their data. Small in itself; the category is not small.

I had read that file end to end three times and not seen it. What found it was handing the finished thing to a separate agent whose only instruction was to break it. And its suggested fix was wrong — the obvious guard would have failed during the very step that fills in the existing rows, which I only knew because I measured it rather than accepting it. The right fix was to stop granting anyone permission to call it directly at all. Reviewing your own work is a different instrument from someone trying to break it, and that third pass is now standing procedure for anything I build myself.

§19.60

Stage 14 · the test I’d argued was unnecessary · fixed · found while building · S14

Eight colours in a palette that has four

My own notes in the change file reasoned, at some length and quite plausibly, that one particular test wasn’t needed. I wrote it anyway. It immediately found that the palette shipped eight colours while the stylesheet defines four — so half the products would have come out with no colour at all, which is exactly the kind of thing nobody notices until a board looks wrong.

Palette narrowed to four, and there’s now a check that fails if the two ever disagree again: widen the stylesheet first, then the palette. An argument that a test is unnecessary is not evidence. The test is.

§19.60

Stage 14 · a refusal that lied about why · fixed · found while building · S14

“That plant doesn’t exist” — about a plant that plainly does

A safety check ran as the person making the edit rather than as the system, so when it went looking for the plant an entry belongs to, it couldn’t see it either. The result was a refusal with entirely the wrong reason attached, and it arrived first, hiding the correct refusal underneath it. Two tests were asserting only “this was refused”, which is why it survived that long.

Fixed, and the lesson is in the tests rather than the code: a refusal test has to check what the refusal says, not just that one happened.

§19.60

Stage 14 · found by walking the edges · fixed · found while building · S14

A site admin could erase their own product’s colour

The colour is filled in automatically when a product is created, but nothing stopped someone clearing it afterwards — leaving a product with no colour and no way back except an admin. One line makes the column require a value; the interesting part is that adding it also changed the verdict on one of the deliberate breakages, which had been reported as harmless and only was harmless once the new rule existed. A verdict on a test has a date on it. The whole table gets re-run after any tightening.

§19.60

Stage 13 · you found it · fixed · you found it · S13

Two screens, one gesture, five separate disagreements

You said the drag styling didn’t match. It didn’t, in five ways: the hover tint was a different colour on each; one had real padding and the other faked padding with a shadow; the grip block was written out twice; the fade was written out twice; and the grip sat in a different place on each screen.

Every line in both files was correct. Nothing was misspelled, nothing was a typo — the defect was that there were two of everything, and two things that are maintained separately drift. One of the two hover colours was the three-unit grey I’d already measured as invisible, so “make them match” was one coin-flip away from making the second screen worse. The answer to a mismatch is never “match them”; it’s “make it impossible to mismatch”, which is the same conclusion this project reached about scaling three weeks ago and apparently had to reach again in a new domain.

§19.58

Stage 13 · a deliberate breakage escaped · fixed · found while building · S13

The check that guards the rule could be switched off without anything going red

The new check works from a list of things a screen isn’t allowed to write for itself. I tried deleting an item from that list to see what happened. Nothing happened. One fewer thing for the screens to violate, one fewer thing for the shared file to provide — so two separate checks both went greener, and the guard quietly covered less.

This is the third time on this project that a list driving a test turned out to be untested itself. There’s now a check that asserts the list, so shortening it goes red in the same run.

§19.58

Stage 13 · only your screenshot could show it · fixed · found while building · S13

The up/down arrows have been rendering blue, and no screenshot I take can see it

In your screenshot the ↑ and ↓ on each level row are blue. Nothing else on that row is. It isn’t a stylesheet bug: those two characters have an emoji form, Windows falls through to an emoji font to draw them, and an emoji glyph brings its own colour — so the grey I set two lines above is simply ignored.

The part worth keeping: every render I have ever taken of that row showed them correctly, because the machine I render on has no emoji font to fall through to. Rendering and looking is the highest-value rule on my list and it could not have found this. Fixed with the character that forces the plain form.

§19.58

Stage 12 · you predicted this one · fixed · you found it · S12

Behind the grip: nearly half of every drag promised a move and did nothing

You wrote — "look for a bug behind the affordance once it is grabbable." There was one, and the reason nobody had found it is exactly the reason you predicted: you cannot find a bug in a control you cannot grab.

With no way to drive your running app, I rendered the real row with the real stylesheet, drove the real code over it at 672 pointer positions, and fed every outcome through the real reordering function. The maths was right: 528 out of 528 drops landed exactly where the green line promised. And 231 of those 528 — 44% — drew a green line that did nothing at all.

Dragging a level, the gap directly above it and the gap directly below it are both places it already is. Two dead zones on every drag. I fixed exactly this on the node tree a fortnight ago; the level list keeps its own copy of the rule and never got the fix. The two screens keeping separate copies of one idea is now the third defect this project has paid for — so the rule has moved to where the reordering function lives, and the piece of arithmetic that used to sit unguarded inside the screen now has 25 checks on it.

§19.56

Stage 12 · found by measuring, not looking · fixed · found while building · S12

The grip has been effectively invisible on both admin screens since the day it shipped

The node tree's drag handle, and the one I was about to copy onto the levels list, are drawn in a grey that measures 1.29 to 1 against the card behind them. Three to one is the floor for something you're meant to find with your eye. On the hover background it measures 1.04 to 1 — it disappears at the exact moment you hover it.

It reads as "subtle" in a screenshot, which is why nobody caught it, and it is why I compute the number now instead of squinting. Shipping the new grip at the old value would have been this stage's own defect wearing a different colour. Both screens fixed together, so they stay identical — being identical is the whole point of the copy.

§19.56

Stage 12 · found by rendering it · fixed · found while building · S12

The new hover tint landed one pixel from the green line and turned it into a border

The green insertion line sits three pixels above its row. The hover tint I'd just added reached four pixels above the same row. So on every drag — because during a drag your pointer is always over some row — the tint's edge landed one pixel from the green line, and the line stopped reading as "it goes between these two" and started reading as "this row has a green top border".

No test could see this: both pieces are correct on their own. The tint now stands down while a drag is live, because once you've grabbed the row the question it was answering is already answered.

§19.56

Stage 15 · the surveys found it · fixed · found while building · S15

A site admin can open three screens whose every edit the server will throw away

Operators, products and shift patterns are still governed by the old company-wide rule. A site admin can edit none of them — and the admin page lets them in. Nothing shows this today only because those three screens don't exist yet.

This is stage 11 read backwards. Stage 11 was anything you hide, the server must also refuse. This is anything you show, the server must also allow — and it's the more dangerous half, because the failure looks like success: the form accepts, the button clicks, and nothing happens. It's why the shared-lists change moved to the front of the queue.

§19.57

Stage 15 · the surveys found it · fixed · found while building · S15

Products don't have a colour — they have a position

There are exactly four product colours and they are handed out by position in a company-wide list. So adding a product re-shuffles the colours of the products you already have, and the fifth product has no colour at all.

This has never mattered because nothing has ever listed products for editing. A Products screen cannot avoid showing a colour, and there isn't a stable one to show — which is why it's a question for you at the top of this page rather than something I quietly pick.

§19.57 · §19.59

Stage 16 · the surveys found it · fixed · found while building · S16

The CSV import is missing a second premise, and creating a site quietly copies a structure

We already knew nodes have no external reference to match an imported row against. The survey found that products have the column and no uniqueness rule on it, so the products half can't match either. Two missing premises, not one.

And a third thing nobody would have looked for: since stage 09, creating a top-level site makes it its own copy of a structure. That is exactly right on a screen where you add one site at a time, and exactly wrong for an import of twelve, where it would silently manufacture twelve structures. The written description of that function still describes how it behaved before stage 09 — so anyone writing this from the documentation would have inherited the error before starting.

§19.57

Stage 11 · checked rather than assumed · fixed · found while building · S11

The app's type definitions are one database change behind

The helper stage 11 added is available to the app and does not appear in the generated file that tells the app what exists. A database change and its regenerated types are supposed to be one change here; this pair has come apart, and nothing has broken yet only because nothing in the app calls it directly.

One command, at the top of this page. I mention it as a finding rather than a chore because the rule it breaks — regenerate in the same sitting — is one I wrote down after it cost a build seven type errors, and it still slipped.

Stage 12 · you found it, twice · fixed · you found it · S12

The level drag was built, unreachable, and marked "not written" in my own plan

You asked for this in the first place; it was built in stage 08. The queue entry for it was never ticked off, so it sat there reading not yet written, blocked for five days after the code shipped. When you said the feature was missing, my own roadmap backed you up. A list nobody reconciles against the disk stops being a plan and becomes a second, confident, wrong answer.

So rather than argue about it, I rendered one level row from its own stylesheet and tested every single pixel against the rule the code itself uses to decide whether a press starts a drag. Green below is everywhere that works:

Green starts a drag. The red line is the middle of the row. Those three green dashes on it are the whole target: 21 pixels of 526.

16% of the row works if you count the hairlines above and below the boxes. Along the middle, where a hand aims, it is 4%. There is no grip and the cursor never changes — the only cursor that changes anywhere on that row is over the buttons, which are the parts you can't drag.

The reason it has no grip is written in the code and it is half right: a finger gets the ↑/↓ arrows instead, so a touch drag doesn't fight the page scroll. True — but the mouse lost its grip in the same stroke, and nothing gave it back. 676 passing checks cannot see an invisible control, because not one of them is aimed at a pixel.

§19.56

You read the plan and found two holes in it · fixed · you found it · S24

There was never a line anywhere asking for a login screen

You thought it would already be on the roadmap. It wasn't. I searched the whole file: the only matches for sign-in are the developer switcher from back in August, and one passing mention in a seed script.

What stood in for it was a single unticked bullet — "auth, profiles, subtree grants, RLS wiring" — and three of those four shipped months' worth of work ago. So the line read as nearly done, and the one part that had not been started at all was the front door. That is how a missing front door hid in plain sight, in a document written specifically to stop things hiding.

It is now four explicit items with the hard part named: the sign-in page is small, invitations need server-side code that does not exist yet, and until both land nobody but you can log in.

P1-6b · P1-6c

Stage 11 · you found it · fixed · you found it · S11

A site admin could remove the company admin

Measured before I touched anything, because the size of a problem decides the size of the fix: it was not an escalation and took nothing away. A company admin's authority lives in a field a site admin cannot write, so the thing being deleted was a redundant duplicate — they still reached every plant afterwards.

It was still wrong three ways. It's a role inversion, the person with less authority editing the record of the person with more. It lies — the button appeared to remove someone's access and removed nothing. And if that person ever lost the company-wide flag, a permission a site admin had quietly deleted would be gone.

Fixed in the database first, then on the screen. Hiding a button the server still allows is a lie of omission, and this whole feature is built on the opposite rule: anything the screen hides, the database must also refuse.

§19.54

Stage 10 · you asked the right question · fixed · you found it · S10

"Why show people if they're not assigned to a plant?"

The list of everybody in the company was there for a reason — it's the pool you add from, and giving a site admin somebody to pick was the entire point of stage 09. But sitting it permanently under the people who actually have access asserts a relationship that doesn't exist, and in a real company it buries the list you came to read under hundreds of strangers.

Adding is now an action. The default view is just the people who can get in; a stranger appears only when you search for one. That also retires a limit I'd written down and hadn't taken seriously enough — that list had no upper bound, so a company of ten thousand would have rendered ten thousand rows.

§19.55

My mistake, and a bad one · fixed · found while building · S10

The demo script hijacked two of the sample company's accounts

I gave the two demo site admins account numbers that were already taken — they belong to the second sample company's admin and to Sofia. So the insert quietly did nothing, the next statement renamed both of those people, and I attached a second company membership to each of them.

Nothing complained, because the database allows one account to belong to two companies. It broke two layers away: signing in returned two memberships where the app expects one, so the session ended up with no membership at all — no Admin link for anybody, and a site admin being told they had no access to a plant they ran.

Every assertion I'd written passed, because they all asked about the demo and none asked whether the numbers were free first. The script now refuses to run if those accounts belong to anyone else, and checks that no demo account ends up in two companies.

§19.53

Stage 10 · found by looking at a picture · fixed · found while building · S10

One row was printing the same sentence twice

A company admin reaches every plant without being given access to any of them, so their row explains that — and then, two columns to the right, explained it again in almost the same words. The second sentence exists to say why there's no "Remove" button; the reason was already sitting next to it.

Shortened to four words. No test could have found this — both sentences were correct, and a suite can't see that they're side by side. It took one screenshot. The check that guards it now asserts the two are different, so it can't come back.

§19.52

Stage 10 · found by looking at a picture · fixed · found while building · S10

People with no email address vanish from a search with nothing said

The search matches on the address, because the address is the only thing this system knows about a person. So an account with no address on file can never be found by typing — it just disappears the moment you search, and nothing tells you it's there.

That's the same failure as silently shortening a list, which the screen already guards against one line higher up. It now counts them and says so.

§19.52

Stage 10 · my own instructions were wrong · fixed · found while building · S10

I told the build agent one line; it was two

There's an audit that stops a stylesheet from shipping without being scaled for large displays, and it keeps a list of the files it covers. My brief said adding the new screen's stylesheet to that list was a one-line change. It also needed a second file updated — a test that hard-codes the same list — and without it the suite would have gone red the moment the required line landed.

The agent found it, fixed it the way that file's own comment says to, and reported it. That's the fourth brief in a row with a real error in it, and the fourth time the agent caught it — which is the argument for writing the brief down rather than just building the thing myself.

§19.52

Stage 09 · found by breaking it on purpose · fixed · a test found it · S09

Three pieces of code I'd written and argued for turned out to do nothing

Each one had a comment next to it explaining why it was needed. The run breaks each piece deliberately and checks whether any test notices — and for these three, nothing did, because something earlier already refused.

Two of them were "read the row back afterwards and make sure the write actually happened", which is a rule I adopted after an earlier bug. It turns out that rule is narrower than I'd been applying it: **it's for writes that can fail quietly.** These can't — the database raises an error rather than silently writing the wrong thing. All three are deleted, with the measurement written down, rather than kept with an explanation nobody can check.

§19.51

Stage 09 · caught by reading, not by running · fixed · found while building · S09

A rule I wrote contradicted its own comment two lines above it

Site admins can't take away their own access to their plant — otherwise they lock themselves out and only a company admin can undo it. The comment I wrote described that narrowly: only the row that makes you the admin of that exact place is protected. The code I wrote refused any change to your own row, including harmless ones like adding yourself as a viewer on one of your own departments, which takes nothing away because the stronger permission above still applies.

No amount of breakage-testing finds this — both versions are perfectly self-consistent. It comes from reading the comment and the code side by side, which is now a standing step.

§19.51

Stage 08 · shipping since the drag first landed · fixed · found while building · S08

The "this row will take it" mark has been drawing itself in the wrong row

The small green mark that says drop it in here has been rendering four pixels down, inside the row below the one it describes. Measured in a browser: the rows sit flush against each other, and the mark was positioned as though there were a gap between them.

It is a piece of styling copied from somewhere it was correct to somewhere it wasn't — the two places measure from different edges. Nobody caught it, including me, twice, because every picture any of us ever made happened to put the target on the last row, where there is no next row to intrude on. My first fix then hid the mark completely inside the row's own green outline — same colour, same pixels. What shipped is the third attempt, and I looked at each one.

§19.49 · D99

A planned change, refused · fixed · found while building · S06

Finishing the last piece as written would have quietly re-opened the safety switch

There's a rule in the database that stops anyone building a tree with a level out of order, and a bypass reserved for migration tools. You asked for that bypass to be locked to the database owner, and it was. My own plan for stage 06 then said to change a nearby function in a way that — measured — hands the bypass back to anyone signed in, because the lock works by asking who is calling and that change makes the answer always "the owner".

as it ships → bypass REFUSED for a signed-in user with the change → bypass ACCEPTED ← your lock undone

So I didn't make it. The problem it was meant to solve — a confusing error message — is fixed a different way, and a test now fails if anyone tries the original change later.

§19.46

Found by running it · fixed · found while building · S06

A site admin couldn't rename their own site

The first thing one of them would try. Rights are stored against a place but used as a path down the tree, so renaming the very place you have rights over moves your own permissions out from under you halfway through the operation.

The obvious fix opens a hole — it also lets a department admin drag their department into a different plant — so the repair is deliberately narrow, and a test proves the narrow version can't be reached any other way. One limitation is left standing and flagged in writing: a department admin still can't rename their own department. A site admin is fine.

§19.46

Worth remembering · fixed · found while building · S06

The rule refusing that rename wasn't the one I was looking at

Opening the write permission completely didn't help. Only opening the read permission did — a row you're allowed to edit but wouldn't then be allowed to see gets refused, and the error names the write rule, not the read rule. Four rounds of bisecting to find, and it means any fix aimed at the obvious rule looks right and changes nothing.

§19.46

Words matter · fixed · you found it

A target computed from the standard is not the standard

The edit pop-up read "Standard for this cell: 12" under the target box. The maintainer: "it is not a standard, it is the target based on the standard. It is very misleading."

They are two different quantities and this feature introduced both. The standard is the cycle time — seconds to make one unit at one cell, set once on the Cycle times screen, and it does not move. The target is what that standard works out to for one assignment's window, and it changes every time the block is resized or its efficiency is edited. Twelve was the second thing wearing the first thing's name.

Left alone it invites a specific mistake: somebody reads "standard for this cell" over a target box, and types the cycle time into it. That saves silently — 90 is a perfectly legal target — and the assignment then carries an explicit target of 90 units that no longer follows a resize, which looks like the derivation quietly breaking.

The pop-up now says "Target from this cell's cycle time: 12", and the chip's tooltip says "(from cycle time)" instead of "(standard)". The prop behind both was called standardQty and is now derivedQty, because the name that misled the reader was also misleading the code. Two tests pin the wording, since a string is exactly what the type checker cannot see: one asserts the pop-up hint never opens with "Standard for this cell", the other that a derived tooltip never uses the word "standard" at all.

Worth noting the shape rather than just the fix: nothing was broken. Every number was right, every test was green, and the defect existed entirely in what the screen called things. It took someone reading the words to find it.

R-316 · R-318

Words matter · fixed · you found it

The settings icon was a sun

The Settings entry in the admin rail drew a circle with eight straight rays radiating out of it. The code even called it a gear — case "settings": // a gear — and it was not one: a gear's teeth are ON its rim, and eight detached spokes around a hub is the universal drawing of a sun. The maintainer read it off the rail exactly that way: "it looks like a mini sun right now, wouldn't the universal gear symbol be more accurate?" It matters most in the COLLAPSED strip, where the icon is the only thing left — a sun there says brightness or weather, and the one section a person hunts for by its conventional symbol is settings. Replaced with a real six-tooth cog outline (one closed path alternating a tip arc with a root arc, plus a hub). Six teeth and not eight because at 16 pixels eight tips and their gaps fall closer together than the stroke is wide and silt up into a plain ring. ⚠️ NO TEST PINS THE SHAPE, deliberately. A path string can be asserted, but what went wrong here was not a wrong string — it was a drawing that did not look like the thing its own comment named, and only a person looking at it can catch that. The evidence is the screenshot, at both rail widths.

R-320

A list that appears twice · open · found while building

The offering rule is written twice, and the two copies are now deliberately different

DEF-0002's fix made productOfferedAt fail CLOSED on a place it cannot resolve. cycleTimes.ts holds a second, hand-inlined copy of the same predicate with its own if (place === undefined) return true, and that copy is RIGHT where it stands: buildCycleGrid's map is built from every node the reader may read, so it is narrowed by permission, not by a view choice — which is exactly why the Cycle times grid was giving the right answer while the board was not. So the two copies must now differ on purpose, and nothing in the tree says so or makes them red together. The rule to apply when reading either: ask what narrowed the map. A permission-narrowed map means "I cannot tell", and the honest answer is to offer; a view-narrowed one means "not here", and the honest answer is to hide. ⚠️ AND THE RULE HAS A LIMIT, FOUND BY DEF-0005 THREE HOURS LATER. It can only be applied by someone who knows what narrowed the map — and for a supervisor BOTH had happened: the board's map was narrowed by a view choice while the product's place list had already been narrowed by PERMISSION, one layer up, before it arrived. By the time a client-side predicate saw an empty array the two were indistinguishable. The product half of the rule is therefore retired rather than refined: migration 0042 moved the question to the server, and scope.ts no longer decides where a part may be run. The rule still governs the operator half (ownedInScope), and the limit is written into the file's header beside it.

R-272 · R-317

A list that appears twice · open · found while building

A third refusal shape nobody can read

Fixing DEF-0003 turned up a THIRD payload shape for the same refusal. move_run (migration 0030, the only definition) raises not_offered_here for a bulk crew check as {kind, node_id, operators} — no id at all, because it is a refusal about MANY operators with the list under operators. The parser still requires id, so that one keeps falling through to Unknown and "Some of this crew do not belong to that part of the structure." never reaches a screen either. This is not the same shape minus a key: deciding what id means for a many-row refusal is a contract decision, which is why it was left rather than folded into DEF-0003's fix. Three shapes now leave the same guard family, which is the duplicated-list hazard in its exact form — the shapes are written in SQL, read in TypeScript, and nothing makes the two red together.

R-046

A test that never ran · fixed · you found it

The CI step that was red was two steps before the one we fixed

The maintainer, on a workflow that had never once been green: "why are my workflows failing on github? I thought we fixed them." DEF-0001 was a real fix and CI never reached it. format:check runs two steps before test and five before e2e, and it had been failing on thirteen committed files — so every run since the trigger was repaired died before the suite, the build or the smoke test started. Nothing about the e2e fix was wrong; it was simply never executed. ⭐ THE MISS IS THE INTERESTING PART, AND IT HAD BEEN WRITTEN DOWN TWICE. The tester reported the red gate in both of its first two runs and both times classified it as "a Prettier gate, and not one of the tracked baseline numbers". True about the NUMBERS, and the wrong conclusion about the WORKFLOW: a step that exits non-zero stops the job whether or not anybody counts it. A fact can be reported accurately, twice, and still not be acted on, because it was filed under the wrong question — "does this move a number we track" rather than "does this stop the build". Fixed by running the repo's own formatter: wrapping and indentation only, in files last touched on 2 and 3 September. One of the fourteen was a hand-authored prose document committed an hour earlier at the top level; it moved into docs/, which .prettierignore already exempts by directory for exactly that kind of file.

R-321

A guard that sits below the place things break · fixed · the tester found it

The fixture the browser sees was the one file no runner ever built

dev_demo.sql builds the world the running app shows - three plants, a line-scoped supervisor, the only fixture that can demonstrate rules the seed's single plant cannot. Migration 0044 dropped runs.status and this file went on writing it, so it died at its runs insert on the next reset. Everything the app needs to LOOK fine was already in by then: the sign-ins worked, the plants were there, and the board was simply empty - which reads as a product with no data rather than as a fixture that stopped half way. ⭐ THE FILE HAD TWELVE ASSERTIONS AND ONE OF THEM WAS THIS BUG. Its section 8 opens by saying, in as many words, that a demo which half-ran is worse than one that failed, and then checks that there are 36 runs. It never ran. Assertions at the FOOT of a file cannot catch a file that dies four hundred lines above them, and that is a general shape rather than an accident of this fixture: a guard placed after the work only fires on the runs that did not need it. So the fix was not more assertions but moving the check to the APPLY, where an error at any line is the failure. ⛔ AND THE REASON IT WAS ON NO RUNNER TURNED OUT TO BE A GOOD ONE, WHICH IS WHY IT LASTED. The file opens by DELETING org 1's seeded content, and about eighteen cases across eight files need that content intact; the SQL suite runs every numbered file against one shared scratch database without rebuilding between them. So it could not simply be given a number - anything sorted after it would have read the wrong world, quietly. The answer was a second database of its own (run-sql-test.sh --demo), not a place in the existing queue.

R-D112 · R-324

A limitation nobody re-read · fixed · found while building

The comment said it was a known edge, and it was the ordinary case

The Trainings list named every row control after the owner's LEAF name -- "Retire Forklift at Line A" -- and trainings.ts recorded the consequence in its own header, in as many words: "it is the LEAF name, so two 'Line 1's in different plants still collide here." Filed as a known edge and read past ever since. ⭐ IT IS NOT AN EDGE, AND THE SCHEMA SAYS SO. nodes carries unique (org_id, parent_id, name): SIBLING names are unique and names across the tree are not. So two plants each naming their first line "Line A" is legal and unremarkable -- it is what people do -- and skills_owner_name_unique then lets each of those lines own a "Forklift". Both rows are permanent, and with one row's edit open four separate controls read identically, including two "Retire Forklift at Line A" buttons of which one retires the wrong plant's course. A screen-reader user has no way to tell them apart. ⚠️ WHAT MAKES THIS WORTH A CARD IS THE SHAPE, NOT THE FIX. The defect was not hidden; it was documented, in the file that caused it, in the words of somebody who had understood it. What was wrong was the label "edge" -- a judgement about how OFTEN it happens, attached to a fact about what the database permits, and never revisited once the product grew a second plant. A comment that excuses a limitation is a place to check, not a place to stop reading. Fixed by naming rows with the owner's full path where the leaf is shared and the leaf alone where it is not -- sibling uniqueness per parent is exactly what makes a whole path unique. The old comment's other half, that a three-level name read out on every button is a cure worse than the disease, was right and is why the short form survives wherever it is unambiguous.

R-258

A command nobody should run · open · found while building

Nine migrations the database has but its own ledger does not

npx supabase migration list --local reports 0037 through 0045 with an empty remote column: the local database's supabase_migrations ledger does not record them as applied. The schema plainly HAS them -- 0044 dropped runs.status, which is exactly why the demo world stopped building (DEF-0006). They were applied by hand rather than through the CLI, and the ledger was never told. ⛔ THE HAZARD IS A COMMAND THAT LOOKS ROUTINE. supabase migration up reads that ledger and would re-run all nine, in order, against a database that already has them. Most are CREATE OR REPLACE and would merely be redundant. 0043 and 0044 are not: they carry ALTER TABLE ... DROP COLUMN and, worse, DELETE FROM assignments WHERE run_id IN (SELECT id FROM runs WHERE status <> 'planned') -- a statement written to run ONCE, before the column went, and now referring to a column that does not exist. The run would fail partway through, and what it did before failing depends on where it got to. ⚠️ WHAT WAS DONE ABOUT IT HERE: nothing, deliberately, beyond not running the command. 0045 was applied by hand the way its neighbours were, which keeps the machine consistent with itself and does not pretend to fix the ledger. Reconciling it properly means deciding whether to mark the nine as applied (supabase migration repair) or to rebuild the database from the migration set, and that is a maintainer's call about their own working database, not something to slip into a session about certifications. ⭐ WHY IT IS FILED RATHER THAN MENTIONED. This is the third time in three sessions that a fact about THIS MACHINE rather than about the code has cost something: the demo fixture on no runner, the phantom table no migration created, and now a ledger that disagrees with the schema. None of them could go red in CI, because none of them exist in a fresh checkout.

R-326

One fact, written twice · open · found while building

The seed spells the same three addresses two different ways

A test hardcoded admin@northwind.example, read straight out of seed.sql's auth.users insert, and went red. The addresses ARE @northwind.example at that point in the file — and the GoTrue dev-login block about 180 lines further down UPDATEs all three to @example.test, which is what any reader of the database actually finds. ⚠️ NEITHER SPELLING IS WRONG AND THAT IS THE PROBLEM. Both are in the same committed file, both look authoritative, and which one is true depends on how far down you read. It is the "column list that appears twice" shape applied to data: one fact, two places, and the second quietly wins. The test now reads every address from the database rather than naming one, which also made its leak checks stronger — "none of the other org's addresses, whatever they happen to be" is a better assertion than a literal. Filed rather than fixed because collapsing the two spellings means editing the seed, and eighteen-odd SQL cases sit on that file.

R-329

A guard the compiler cannot give you · open · found while building

The generated types promise a string where the database may send nothing

audit_actor_identities() returns email text — nullable, because a profile can exist whose auth row carries no address. supabase gen types cannot see nullability THROUGH a RETURNS TABLE signature, so the generated type says email: string. tsc will therefore accept identity.email.toLowerCase() and it will throw at runtime on the first person without an address. ⭐ IT IS THE §19.76 SHAPE WITH A NEW ROAD IN. That rule is "a migration that makes a column NULLABLE is a client change db:types will not surface, so grep every guard". Here nothing was made nullable — the value was BORN nullable, in a function's return signature, where the generator has never been able to see it. The same silence, arrived at from the other direction. Handled for now by parseActorIdentity, which normalises the value and is pinned by a case, so the lie never escapes the api layer. Left OPEN because the generated file still says the wrong thing and the next person to read database.types.ts will believe it.

R-329

A screen offering what the server refuses · open · found while building

The board offers people whose certificates have expired, and the save then fails

Checking a queue item against the code rather than reading its line, ahead of choosing what to do next. The item claimed certification expiry still needed wiring to the board. The SERVER half has been there since 0009: check_eligibility computes expiring_skills as expires_at < upper(p_timerange) and eligible is false when any exist, and create_assignment acts on it — refusing outright under block, and under warn refusing unless an override is supplied. ⛔ THE BOARD ASKS A SIMPLER QUESTION AND GETS A DIFFERENT ANSWER. CreatePopover computes ineligible from !o.skillIds.includes(s.id) — does this person hold the training at all — and never looks at expires_at. So a person whose certificate lapsed a year ago is drawn as eligible, raises no warning, and is offered no override tick. Create then fails with "operator is not eligible for this node/window; override required under warn policy". Because the client never decided an override was needed, the box that would supply one is not on screen: the assignment cannot be completed through the board at all. A dead end, not a warning. ⚠️ THE PARTS ARE ALL PRESENT, WHICH IS WHY THIS IS SMALL AND WHY IT LASTED. shapes.ts already parses expiring_skills into expiringSkills, with its own type; grep for it under src/features/board/ and there is not one hit. The data arrives at the client and stops there. ⭐ THE SHAPE IS SECTION 4'S RULE WITH THE ARROW REVERSED. That rule warns against a screen that shows what the server will refuse; this is a screen that OFFERS what the server refuses, which is the same disagreement and the worse half, because the person only finds out after doing the work.

R-330

A constraint that passes on nothing · fixed · found while building

The CHECK that guards the eligibility policy cannot reject a null

Migration 0001 guards the org settings bag with check (settings->>'eligibility_policy' in ('warn','block')), and that reads like it admits exactly two values. It admits three. ⛔ NULL IN ('warn','block') IS NULL, NOT FALSE, AND A CHECK CONSTRAINT PASSES ON NULL. Postgres rejects a row only when a check evaluates to FALSE; unknown is accepted, which is the documented behaviour and the whole reason NOT NULL is a separate thing to write. So jsonb_build_object('eligibility_policy', NULL) stores a JSON null, settings->>'…' reads back SQL NULL, the check evaluates to unknown, and the write is allowed. The setting is then absent, every server-side COALESCE(…, 'warn') quietly answers warn, and a plant that had been set to block is silently permissive again. Verified rather than reasoned about: select null::text in ('warn','block') is null is true on this database. ⭐ IT WAS FOUND BY SABOTAGE, WHICH IS THE POINT. The lane building set_org_eligibility_policy removed its own IN test to check that its case could fail — and TWO cases went red, the expected one and a second showing the stored value as NULL. That second failure is the one nobody was looking for. A test written only to prove the happy path would not have gone near it. Closed by the function's own p_policy IS NULL test, which is therefore load-bearing rather than belt-and-braces. ⚠️ The CHECK itself is unchanged and still cannot reject a null — anything that writes the bag by another route inherits the hole, and migrations are append-only, so this is recorded rather than repaired.

R-330

A limit you can measure before you hit it · open · found while building

The Activity plant filter has a ceiling, and it is a request line

audit_log has no place column, so narrowing the Activity log to a plant means asking the server about ids held INSIDE the snapshot jsonb — and the clause has to repeat that id list once per snapshot path (after->>node_id, after->>site_node_id, and both again for before). ⛔ SO THE FILTER'S COST GROWS WITH THE COMPANY, AND THE LIMIT IS A REQUEST LINE RATHER THAN A QUERY. Kong caps it at 8 KB. Measured against this stack with a realistic page request beside it: a 7328-character clause is served and 7624 comes back 414. The budget is set at 6800 — roughly 44 places outside the chosen plant. Each of the four plants here leaves 36, so it works today with about eight nodes of headroom, and a fifth plant or a deeper tree spends that. ⭐ WHAT IT DOES AT THE CEILING IS THE PART THAT MATTERS. It does not silently read every plant under a footer naming one. The screen says it is showing every plant and hiding nothing, the plant is dropped from the footer so the sentence cannot claim a scope it does not have, and the read THROWS rather than going unnarrowed. Degrading loudly is the whole reason this is a finding and not a defect. The durable fix is a node_id column on audit_log, written by write_audit_log from the row's own node or site column: one indexed in instead of four id lists, and no ceiling worth naming. ⚠️ It only helps rows written AFTER it, so the 232 rows already here would stay unattributed unless a backfill reads each snapshot — which is a migration, and a decision about a table that is deliberately append-only history.

R-334 · R-330

A fact that expired while it was being used · fixed · you found it

Two correct lanes, one stale sentence between them

The maintainer, on the screen: "why is the date column in activity tab not honoring the selected date format for that plant?" Because it asked for the company's. ⛔ NEITHER LANE WAS WRONG, AND THAT IS THE POINT. Two ran in parallel in one session. One made the date format per-plant and was briefed — by me, in writing — that "Activity spans plants and therefore reads the COMPANY value", which was true when the brief was written. The other gave Activity a plant filter, which made it false while the first was still building. Each shipped correct work against a premise the other was quietly retiring, and the requirement I wrote recorded the expired version as though it were a property of the screen rather than a state it happens to be in. ⚠️ NO TEST COULD HAVE CAUGHT IT AND THAT IS NOT AN OVERSIGHT. Each lane's cases pinned its own half and both stayed green; the full suite passed at 2097. The contradiction lived in prose — in a brief and in a requirement — and there is nothing in this repo that reads a sentence and a screen together. The seam that DID exist, useDateFormat's optional plant argument, was built and simply never handed anything by this screen. ⭐ WHAT IT CHANGES ABOUT RUNNING LANES IN PARALLEL. Splitting by FILE is what makes parallel lanes safe, and it worked — no lane touched another's files. But a shared FACT is not a file, and a brief that states one as settled hands every lane a copy that cannot be invalidated. The cheap guard is to name, in each brief, which premises the OTHER lanes are allowed to change, and to re-read them at integration rather than only re-running the tests. Fixed by passing the chosen plant, with two cases: the date column is resolved for the chosen plant, and still falls back to the company answer on All plants. The first was checked against the bug put back on purpose. The test seam now records what useDateFormat was ASKED, because a stub that ignored its arguments would let a panel resolving the company answer pass every case in the file — which is exactly the state that shipped.

R-333

A rule that expired without anyone editing it · fixed · the tester found it

The tab was built for her, tested for her, and she could not open it

Dana runs Plant A. Every admin tab is hers except Settings, which is where a plant's date format and its eligibility rule are set --- her plant's rules, and she could not reach them. ⭐ NOBODY WROTE THE BUG. companyAdminOnly: true on the Settings row was CORRECT when it was written: 0037 shipped Settings as one org-wide preference, and a site admin opening it could only be told no. Migration 0050 added per-plant settings with the gate app_is_admin() OR app_is_admin_for(node), 0052 moved the date format under the same shape, and at that moment the flag became false --- with no edit to the file that holds it, and nothing in the diff of either migration pointing at it. The comment above it went on explaining, fluently and in the present tense, a reason that had stopped applying. ⛔ THE TEST THAT WAS SUPPOSED TO STOP THIS IS WHAT HELD IT IN PLACE. auditAccess.test.tsx ended with a case named "Settings and Activity are gated by the same test, so neither can drift", asserting a site admin got NEITHER tab. The tie was deliberate and, when written, right --- the two tabs did answer the same question. 0050 made them different questions, and the case went on asserting the old answer in green. This is CLAUDE.md §4's "a green case can be pinning the bug" in its purest form: the guard against drift, still green, WAS the drift, and its name is the reason it was worth reading twice. ⭐ AND THE PANEL BEHIND IT WAS ALREADY FINISHED. SettingsPanel's header describes three site-admin states --- company scope disabled with the reason, their own plant with live pickers, a readable-but-not-administered plant with no control and whose place it is --- canAdministerPlant implements all three, and settingsPanel.test.tsx has cases for each. Forty cases on a requirement whose audience could not open the screen. The fix was one flag; the work had been done and was simply behind a locked door. ⚠️ WHAT WOULD HAVE CAUGHT IT, AND IT IS NOT MORE TESTS. Every suite was green at 2103 and stayed 2103 through the fix --- one case changed its assertion, none was added. What found it was a person signing in AS Dana and reading the rail, then asking the server the same question over her own session with a deliberately illegal value, so the permission gate had to answer first and nothing was written: invalid_argument, not not_permitted. The probe is the technique worth keeping --- it separates "the gate refused you" from "the gate passed you and something else stopped it", which is precisely the distinction a hidden control destroys. ⚠️ THE STANDING LESSON. CLAUDE.md §4 already says a migration making a column NULLABLE is a client change db:types will not surface. This is the same shape one level up: A MIGRATION THAT WIDENS A PERMISSION GATE IS A CLIENT CHANGE NOTHING SURFACES. After any change to who may call a writer, grep the client for the constant that used to mirror it. And where the server's gate takes a NODE, the client's mirror cannot be a boolean on a menu row.

R-239 · R-333 · R-335

A guard that was only ever a spare · fixed · the tester found it

The lock I removed was doing a second job

Nothing a user could see was wrong. The rail was correct for all four roles on the running app. What was missing was any way for it to STOP being correct: offering a supervisor with no admin grant the Import, Products and Settings tabs --- one line in adminSectionsFor --- passed all 2103 cases. ⛔ AND IT IS A CONSEQUENCE OF THE PREVIOUS FIX, WHICH IS WHY IT IS WORTH A CARD. Before 23ba9aa the Settings tab had TWO locks, adminSectionsFor and companyAdminOnly, and a widening of the first was still stopped by the second. DEF-0007 removed the second, correctly --- it was answering a per-node question on a menu row. But a redundant lock is also a SPARE, and nothing checked whether the remaining one was tested before the spare was taken away. It was not. The defect is not in the fix; it is in what the fix stopped covering, and that is a question worth asking every time something is deleted for being redundant: redundant with WHAT, and is that thing pinned? ⛔ THE CASE THAT LOOKED LIKE THE GUARD WAS CATCHING IT BY ACCIDENT. adminNoGrants N3 asserts which PANEL a supervisor lands on, and resolveAdminSection falls back to the first allowed section --- so allowing "access" changes the landing panel and N3 goes red, while allowing anything sorting after "operators" in SECTIONS (products, cycle times, import, settings) leaves it alone and N3 stays green. One widening caught by coincidence, the rest invisible. A test that fails for a reason other than the one its name implies is worse than no test, because it is counted. ⚠️ AND A COMMENT SAID THE CASES EXISTED. requireAdmin.test.tsx read "that is a different question and has its own cases". There were none. That sentence was a PLAN when it was written and read like a RECORD ever after --- the same failure as DEF-0007's stale comment, one day later and in a test file rather than a component. Both are now pointers that can be checked: a file name and a case number, which either resolve or do not. ⭐ THE FIX IS THE ASSERTION SHAPE, NOT MORE CASES. Three toBeTruthy() checks can be widened quietly; the whole list in the rail's own order cannot. N6 asserts that list for the narrowed population, N7 for a company admin so N6 cannot pass by the rail emptying, and N6 was measured red against the tester's own mutation before being called done.

R-335 · R-239

A record that kept half its columns · fixed · you found it

The buildout stopped recording the shape of the work while recording everything else

The maintainer, opening the page: "Please check the scheduler buildout, I don't think it has been updated." It had not. Thirty requirements --- R-306 to R-335, every one of them shipped and covered --- belonged to no stage. The highest requirement any stage claimed was R-305, and the stage list stopped at S25 while sessions ran on to 68. ⭐ THE OTHER THREE HALVES OF THE FILE WERE FINE, WHICH IS WHY NOBODY NOTICED. requirements, findings and sessions were all current --- every piece of work in that range had a session entry, a requirement row and a test named against it. What was missing was only the layer that says what BELONGS WITH WHAT: which of those thirty rows were one job. A reader could learn that set_node_setting is gated per node and find the cases proving it, and still not learn that the Settings tab was a piece of work with a beginning and an end. ⚠️ AND THE SHAPE OF THE OMISSION EXPLAINS ITSELF. CLAUDE.md section 3 lists what closes a piece of work and sessions is FIRST; the stage's status/state is second, and is the only item that requires deciding whether the thing you just did was part of something larger. A session entry is always writable at the end of a session. A stage card is not, so it kept being the thing that could wait --- for thirteen sessions, and shipped: [] on every one of them was the visible symptom nobody read as one. ⛔ THE QUEUE HAD GONE STALE THE SAME WAY, AND THAT ONE COULD HAVE MISLED SOMEBODY INTO REBUILDING WORKING CODE. "A way to CHOOSE warn or block for eligibility" was still listed as next. Its own note said what was missing was a Settings control plus one write RPC --- which is exactly what shipped in sessions 62-63. A finished feature sitting at the top of a queue is worse than an empty queue: it is an instruction. ⭐ WHAT IT CHANGES. The eight stages added here (S26-S33) were grouped from the requirements' own claims and the sessions that shipped them, and the grouping is a judgement --- it is the developer's reading of what was one job, and is meant to be argued with rather than inherited. The durable part is smaller and duller: shipped on a session is not decoration, it is the only link between a day's work and the shape it belongs to, and an empty one should be as uncomfortable to leave as an empty numbers.

S26 · S32

The ledger

Every requirement, and what proves it

One row per thing the product must do. Covered means a test in the suite is named for it; uncovered means nothing but a person checks it; contradicted means an open defect shows the code doing something else.

278 requirements · 158 covered · 105 uncovered · 1 contradicted · 14 superseded
R-D1 Profiles reference auth.users by uuid
user_profiles.user_id is a uuid foreign key to auth.users with ON DELETE CASCADE and a unique (org_id, user_id).

R-D2 The capacity cap is read from org settings, default 1.0
check_operator_capacity reads orgs.settings capacity_cap, defaulting to 1.0. Configurability is proven by the 1.1-peak case, refused at cap 1.0 and accepted at 1.2.

R-D3 Child rows reference their parent by composite (org_id, id)
Parents carry unique (org_id, id) and children reference (org_id, id), so cross-tenant row stitching is structurally impossible.

R-D4 Two runs on one cell may not overlap in time
runs carries an exclusion constraint on (node_id, timerange) for non-cancelled rows, so a run move onto an occupied window is refused by the database with run_overlap.

R-D5 An assignment has exactly one of run_id or product_id
A CHECK enforces exactly one of run_id and product_id, and a trigger forces a run-attached assignment's node_id to equal its run's node, refusing a half-completed cross-cell move.
§17, D5 · agent		 — uncovered
R-D6 nodes.path is derived by trigger and cascades on rename or move
Callers never set path. A trigger slugifies the name onto the parent path and an AFTER UPDATE OF name, parent_id trigger rewrites every descendant's path.
§17, D6, §17.1 · agent		 — uncovered
R-D7 org_id is carried on every child table for uniform RLS
shifts, shift_breaks, node_shift_templates, operator_skills and node_skill_requirements all carry org_id.

R-D8 Operators, products, skills readable org-wide; nodes and writes grant-scoped
Under RLS any user in the org can read operators, products and skills, so a conflict message can name a cell outside their grant. Subtree grants restrict which nodes are visible and all writes.

audit_log rows are written by a SECURITY DEFINER trigger on runs and assignments only, are readable only by admins, and a no-op update (only updated_at changed) writes no row.

Horizontal position is minutes since the window start over a computed windowMinutes. Shift geometry loops from day −1 so an overnight shift from the day before the window appears on the first morning.

R-D74 Hierarchy RPCs raise named codes, never raw constraint errors
path_collision, node_cycle, level_mismatch, level_in_use, node_in_use and schedulable_level_locked join the closed set (twelve codes in all), every one PT409. RPCs pre-check and raise a named code, and malformed arguments such as NULL or an unparseable id return a closed-set code rather than 23502 or 22P02.

Anything outside the fitted scroll container that consumes vertical space (header, toolbar, app shell, status lines) scales with the viewport-driven --chrome-scale. --ui-scale reaches only fitted content. Otherwise fit feeds back into its own input and oscillates.

A product stores its colour as a palette token name, never a hex; the column is NOT NULL and is filled on insert with the least-used token within the owner's scope, so inserting a product never changes an existing product's colour. A product or shift pattern can belong to any node in the hierarchy, not only a plant. R-D107 Ownership decides who may read, not only who may edit
A shared row is readable when it is company-wide, or when its owning node and one of the reader's grants lie on the same branch in either direction — a grant on Line 1 reads Plant 1's rows, a grant on Plant 2 does not. Company admins read everything. Dependents follow their parent: shifts their template, breaks their shift, operator_skills the operator. The org_id term stays inside every policy.

R-D108 There is no company-wide row
site_node_id is NOT NULL on products, operators, skills and shift_templates. The read rule is one sentence: you may read a row when the node that owns it and one of your grants are on the same branch, either direction; company admins read everything. No trigger fills a default owner — a client must send one.

R-D109 Ownership scopes any level; nothing is assigned outside it
A row may be owned by any node, not only a root. A run's product must be owned by an ancestor-or-self of the run's node; likewise an assignment's operator, a node's training requirements and a node's shift pattern. The refusal is not_offered_here. Under this rule the history read exception is provably redundant and was deleted.

R-D110 Delete keeps the past
Deleting a product or person removes it from the list and from anything not yet started; completed and started runs keep their record of it. "Not yet started" is lower(timerange) > now(); a run whose start is unbounded is kept. The dialog names the counts and encourages Deactivate first; it is the site admin's call.

R-326 A certification cannot outlive the plant it was earned in
The maintainer, asked whether cross-plant certifications should be possible at all: "I do not want cross-plant certifications." app_guard_operator_skill_scope already refuses to CREATE a holder row whose training and person sit on different branches; the same test must be applied when either END moves. Moving a training to an owner that no longer covers one of its holders is refused, and so is moving a person away from a training they hold. ⚠️ The test is the INSERT guard's own -- containment in EITHER direction -- and not app_owner_covers_in_org, which is one-directional: a person owned by a whole plant may legitimately hold a training owned by one of its lines, and a rehome guard using the narrower test would refuse a move that leaves a pairing the insert guard permits.

R-330 A filtered log says what it searched, never what it did not
"It needs a filter for time period and then on the what column as well." The Activity list filters by period, kind of change and kind of thing. ⛔ THE LIST IS KEYSET-PAGED, so the screen keeps the SCAN (rows read) and the MATCH (rows kept) apart and may only say "all" when it can prove the scan passed the period's edge — rows inside a now-anchored period are a PREFIX of a newest-first read, so one row older than the cutoff proves nothing unread can match. Periods that do not end at now are therefore not offered at all. The cutoff is frozen when chosen, and a row whose timestamp cannot be parsed is always shown and never counted as proof the window is exhausted.

R-D113 Placing a person outside their area needs a recorded reason
Anyone who can schedule at a cell (app_can_edit_node) may place someone outside their area by setting assignments.area_override with an area_override_reason. It is its own flag and reason, separate from the training override, so waving through a missing training never also places someone in an area they are not cleared for. The trigger honours it on every write path — create_assignment, move_run, apply_split_coverage and a raw PATCH — and normalises the flag off when the row did not need it. move_run pre-checks and names every affected person. The product half has no override.
